Color Blindness

12 pages · photo · narrator: Clear adult man — An explainer would tell this aloud, clearly walking through how color vision works and what color vision deficiency is.
1
Choosing a red crayon is much easier for a person with full-color vision (left) than for a person with red-green color blindness (right). What Colors Do You See? Imagine looking at a box of crayons and seeing only shades of gray, blue, and yellow, or looking at a traffic signal on which the red and green lights look very similar. That’s how the world can appear to someone with color blindness. They see the world differently than people with full-color vision. Color blindness is the world’s most common genetic condition, affecting more than ten million people in the United States alone. Despite its name, though, color blindness does not cause blindness, so the medical community now calls the condition color vision deficiency (CVD). Most people affected by CVD can still see some colors. CVD simply means the inability or decreased ability to see certain colors.
Picture: On the left, a person with full-color vision chooses a red crayon from a box; on the right, a person with red-green color blindness looks at the same crayons. Nearby, a traffic signal shows red and green lights that can look very similar.
2
Where Do Colors Come From? It’s easier to understand CVD when you realize that colors are actually different wavelengths of light. If you use a prism to separate white light into a rainbow, you’re actually separating the white light into its different wavelengths. The red end of the spectrum contains longer wavelengths of light while shorter wavelengths of light are at the purple end. Objects appear to be different colors because they absorb and reflect different wavelengths of light. For example, a ripe strawberry looks red because it reflects long (red) wavelengths. If it instead reflected short wavelengths of light, it would look blue! The flower’s petals look red because they reflect long (red) wavelengths, while its leaves look green because they reflect medium (green) wavelengths.
Picture: A prism splits white light into a rainbow spectrum, with longer red wavelengths at one end and shorter purple wavelengths at the other. A ripe strawberry and a flower with red petals and green leaves show how objects reflect different wavelengths of light.
3
How an Eye Sees How Color Vision Works Humans see colors because light-detecting cells in our eyes respond differently to different wavelengths of light. When light enters one of your eyes, the lens focuses it on the retina, a thin layer of tissue at the back of the eyeball. There it activates light-sensitive cells, called rod cells and cone cells. Activated cells send electrical signals to the brain through the optic nerve, where they are translated into images.
Picture: A cutaway view of an eye shows light entering and the lens focusing it on the retina at the back. Rod cells and cone cells in the retina activate and send electrical signals through the optic nerve to the brain, where the signals become images.
4
Rods and Cones Rod cells are the most sensitive cells in your retina. They allow you to see in extremely dim light, but they can’t tell different colors apart. Cone cells are responsible for color vision. Each cell contains one of three different light-absorbing molecules, called photopigments, which detect blue, green, or red light. Combining these three main colors allows you to see thousands of colors. When one or more photopigments aren’t functioning properly, though, CVD ensues. Color Vision in Animals Most animals can see fewer colors than humans. For instance, dogs can only see shades of blue and yellow; bulls cannot see the color red; and other animals—including some birds, bees, and butterflies—see more colors than humans do, including ultraviolet and infrared wavelengths of light that are invisible to humans. Animals use the information they gather from colors around them to communicate with each other, hunt prey for food, and defend themselves from predators.
Picture: A close view compares rod cells and cone cells, showing photopigments that detect blue, green, or red light. Nearby, a dog sees shades of blue and yellow, a bull is shown with the color red, and a bird, bee, and butterfly see colors beyond human vision.
5
Different Types of CVD Red-Green CVD Red-green CVD, which causes difficulty when it comes to telling the difference between red and green, is the most common type of color vision condition. It’s hereditary, found most often in Caucasian people of northern European ancestry. It’s also more common in men, of which it affects one out of twelve, than in women, of which it affects only one in two hundred. Red-green CVD is usually caused by defects in the green photopigment. Without the green photopigment, green and yellow objects appear reddish in color. Defects in the red photopigment also cause red-green CVD. In this case, red, orange, and yellow objects appear green or black. Although people with red-green CVD have difficulty distinguishing colors, they can still see clearly because red and green cones have some overlap in the wavelengths of light they detect. Their eyes can still detect incoming light red-green CVD vision even if one type of cone is missing or malfunctioning.
Picture: A comparison shows red and green objects side by side as seen with full-color vision and with red-green CVD, where the colors become hard to tell apart. The condition is hereditary, more common in men than in women, and defects in green or red photopigments change how red, orange, yellow, and green appear.
6
Blue-Yellow CVD Blue-yellow CVD occurs when the blue photopigment is malfunctioning or is missing. It’s fairly rare, only occurring in about one out of every ten thousand people worldwide and, unlike red-green CVD, it’s equally common in men and women. Some people with blue-yellow CVD have no blue cone cells at all, which causes them to see blue as green and to see yellow as violet or gray. More commonly, people who have blue-yellow CVD have partially functional blue cone cells and, as a result, have difficulty distinguishing yellow and red from pink.
Picture: A side-by-side comparison shows blue-yellow CVD: a blue object may look green, and yellow may appear violet or gray. People with partially functional blue cone cells have trouble telling yellow and red from pink, while full-color vision shows the colors distinctly.
7
Complete CVD Complete CVD is the most severe form of CVD. This condition occurs when there are defects in two or more of the three types of cones. You might think that a single kind of cone would be enough to enable color vision, but you’d be mistaken. The brain must Some traffic lights use shapes as from at least two well as colors to convey information to drivers with CVD. of cone cells to identify colors. That means people who have only blue cone cells, for example, are able to see blue wavelengths of light, but they can’t distinguish any colors. People who are missing two or more types of cones tend to be particularly sensitive to light and may also have difficulty seeing clearly, especially in bright light. That’s because they have to rely on rod cells for most or all of their vision, and rod cells only function in dim lighting conditions. Only about one out of thirty thousand people worldwide are missing all three types of cones. People who lack any functioning cone cells see the world in shades of black, white, and gray.
Picture: A person with complete CVD is shown beside traffic lights that use shapes as well as colors. A view of the world in shades of black, white, and gray shows what people missing all three types of cone cells see, while another example shows sensitivity to bright light.
8
The Challenges of CVD People with CVD may face some challenges. For example, they may have trouble reading color- coded information on maps, graphs, and charts. Children with CVD may have challenges with classwork, especially if their CVD has not been diagnosed. Red-green CVD makes it difficult to read yellow chalk on a green chalkboard, and all types of CVD make it difficult to choose the correct colors for art and science projects. CVD can also cause safety issues. Fire hydrants, car warning lights, and emergency equipment are often colored red or yellow to make them more visible. These bright colors may be obvious to those with full-color vision but unnoticeable to those with CVD. People with this condition may also have difficulty noticing sunburns, rashes, or undercooked meat. In some cases, CVD may be a factor when a person chooses what career to pursue. Some positions, such as geologist or commercial airline pilot, require full-color vision. Other careers, such as interior designer, photographer, and food inspector, are significantly more challenging without full-color vision. Still, people with CVD can usually work around those challenges to succeed at almost any job.
Picture: A child works at a green chalkboard with yellow chalk, while maps and graphs use color-coded information that is hard to read. A fire hydrant and car warning lights stand out in red or yellow, and a person considers careers such as airline pilot or food inspector.
9
Testing for CVD The Ishihara Test is the most common test for red-green color vision conditions. It uses images composed of dots that are different sizes and colors. People with full-color vision can see numbers or shapes inside the picture, but people with red-green CVD may find it difficult or impossible to identify the hidden symbols. Other, similar tests are used to detect yellow- blue CVD. Can you see the hidden numbers or shapes in the circles in this Ishihara Test?
Picture: A set of Ishihara test plates made of dots in different sizes and colors fills the scene. Inside some circles, hidden numbers or shapes are visible to people with full-color vision, while people with red-green CVD may find them difficult or impossible to identify.
10
These special EnChroma allow to see a much world when they wear them. Tools to Help People with CVD Currently, there’s no known cure for CVD, but modern technology has led to the development of many new tools to help people with this condition. Smartphone apps can perform helpful tasks, such as identifying colors and recommending complementary colors. These apps can even help people choose matching clothes or tell which fruits are ripe at the grocery store. Some smartphone apps can only identify colors after the user snaps a photo. Others can analyze the colors in the camera’s viewfinder without requiring the user to take a photo first, which allows users to identify colors in real time. Researchers have also created high-tech tinted lenses that filter out specific wavelengths of light. Special sunglasses that use these lenses allow people with red-green CVD to distinguish between the two colors.
Picture: A person with red-green CVD wears special tinted sunglasses and sees a much more colorful world. Nearby, a smartphone app identifies colors in a camera viewfinder, helping choose matching clothes or tell which fruits are ripe at the grocery store.
11
Treating CVD Although no true treatment for CVD currently exists, that may soon change because scientists were able to find A squirrel monkey takes a color test. a workaround for red-green CVD in squirrel monkeys recently. The monkeys lacked the red photopigment and, as a result, couldn’t tell the difference between red and green. Researchers used a technology called gene therapy to change traits in the monkeys. They engineered a specially constructed virus to insert the red photopigment gene into the monkeys’ green cone cells. Within about twenty weeks, treated monkeys could distinguish between red and green dots. This was exciting news because the monkeys had lived with CVD since birth. Many doctors and scientists had predicted that it would be impossible for them to see in full color because their brains would not have developed the connections needed to interpret color information, connections that usually develop when animals are young. Now that we know that there is a work-around for CVD in adult squirrel monkeys, there’s hope for people who may want to have full-color vision, too.
Picture: A squirrel monkey takes a color test with red and green dots. Researchers used gene therapy, inserting the red photopigment gene into the monkey’s green cone cells with a specially constructed virus; within about twenty weeks, treated monkeys could distinguish red from green.
12
Living with CVD CVD is a fairly common condition that can be inherited by anyone. Perhaps you or someone in your family has CVD or knows someone who does. Detecting CVD early can help people understand and adjust to what they are seeing. Though CVD adds some challenge to certain activities, most people with CVD get along just fine. They may see the world a little bit differently, but that doesn’t stop them from having rich, fulfilling lives.
Picture: A person with CVD goes about everyday activities, showing that the condition is common and can be inherited. Early detection helps people understand and adjust, and most people with CVD live rich, fulfilling lives even though they see the world a little differently.

Stonehenge

16 pages · drawing · narrator: British man — A historian would tell this aloud, explaining the ancient site and the evidence researchers use to understand it.
1
Stonehenge is one of the most popular prehistoric sites in the world. Stonehenge is one of the most popular prehistoric sites in the world. A Monument from Before Written History Thousands of years ago, a community of people went to great effort to raise a ring of massive stones in southern England. Today, this monument is known as Stonehenge. As the centuries have passed, many of these huge megaliths have fallen over. Still, it has remained a place of fascination long after people forgot its original purpose. About nine hundred years ago, an English historian, Geoffrey of Monmouth, tried to explain the mysterious monument. He told a fanciful story about Merlin, the mythical wizard of the King Arthur legends. He wrote that Merlin advised another king to create a memorial that would stand forever. The result was Stonehenge. Monmouth claimed it was built with the aid of Merlin’s magic about sixteen hundred years ago.
Picture: A wide view of the ring of huge standing stones at Stonehenge on a grassy plain in southern England, many of the massive megaliths leaning and toppled. In a legendary scene, a robed, bearded wizard, Merlin, stands with a king, advising him to raise a memorial that will stand forever.
2
For hundreds of years, archaeologists, historians, and scientists have been investigating Stonehenge, seeking scientific explanations instead of supernatural ones. Archaeologists have excavated the site for clues about its history, builders, and purpose. What researchers have learned is just as remarkable as any legend of kings, wizards, and magic. Scholars once believed Stonehenge was approximately two thousand years old, but they were stunned when research revealed its builders had begun work on it three thousand years before that. This was during the Neolithic period (10,000–2500 bc), a time before the people in Britain had developed metalworking or even invented the wheel. Archaeologists Archaeologists investigate the origin investigate the origin of the bluestones, a of the bluestones, a rare type of stone used rare type of stone used at Stonehenge. at Stonehenge.
Picture: Archaeologists kneel in an open trench at Stonehenge, uncovering rough bluestones with small tools and brushes, soil heaped beside them. Others measure and record the stones, seeking scientific clues about the monument's builders, its history and its purpose.
3
United Kingdom SCOTLAND ★ NORTHERN IRELAND ★ IRELAND ENGLAND ★ Stone Age Site Stonehenge is built of earth and huge rocks. It is not towering like the Washington Monument or enormous like the great pyramids of Egypt. Instead, it owes much of its majesty to the mystery of its ancient origins. As visitors approach Stonehenge, located on a grassy plain in southern England, its massive standing stones gradually come into view. The monument consists of circles built inside circles. The outer ring is a ditch that surrounds an embankment of raised soil measuring about 110 meters (360 ft.) in diameter. In the center stands the famous ring of stones, many of which are now missing.
Picture: Visitors walk a path towards Stonehenge on a wide grassy plain in southern England as the massive standing stones slowly come into view. Seen from above, the monument shows circles inside circles: a low ditch and raised bank of earth surrounding the central ring of stones, many now missing.
4
Different kinds of stones are used at Stonehenge. The standing sandstone slabs that form the outer ring are called sarsen stones, the largest of which weighs about 36,000 kilograms (79,366 lb.). Stone lintels lie across the tops of the standing stones and once connected all of them together. Smaller boulders called bluestones lie within the sarsen circle. They are made of a type of rock called dolerite, a coarse basalt rock. The largest bluestone weighs about 2,994 kilograms (6,600 lb.). Inside the ring of sarsen stones is another group of standing stones, set in a horseshoe shape. These stones form trilithons, square arches with two standing stones and one lintel lying across. They measure approximately 6.7 meters (22 ft.) across and 9 meters (29.5 ft.) tall. An artist shows what Stonehenge might have looked like when it was complete.
Picture: A close view of the stones: tall sandstone sarsen slabs stand in a ring with flat lintels lying across their tops, and smaller bluestones rest inside the circle. Deeper in, paired standing stones carry a single lintel, forming square arches. An artist's picture shows the whole monument complete, every stone in place.
5
Stonehenge • Today, seventeen sarsen stones mark the curve of the Sarsen Circle, but archaeologists believe there were thirty at one time. • The large blocks average 22,680 kilograms (50,000 lb.)— more than a school bus—and stand more than 5 meters (16.4 ft.) tall. • Free-standing stones, including the Heel Stone and Slaughter Stone, are part of the monument but lie outside of the Sarsen Circle. • Some stones may have been dragged off over the last few thousand years, and others likely sank into the ground. • Important rituals were likely staged at Stonehenge—crowning rulers, celebrating harvests, or honoring dead ancestors. Some experts believe only high priests or other religious leaders were allowed inside the sacred circle of stones.
Picture: Seventeen weathered sarsen stones curve across the grass, with the free-standing Heel Stone and Slaughter Stone lying outside the ring. Inside the sacred circle, robed high priests and religious leaders stand apart while a crowd gathers beyond the stones for a ritual, perhaps crowning a ruler or celebrating a harvest.
6
A Landscape of Monuments Stonehenge sits on Salisbury Plain, an area of rolling hills and grasslands. Evidence suggests people have been living there for more than ten thousand years. During the Neolithic period, Salisbury Plain was wooded and filled with animal life. It was a place of plenty for people who lived as hunter-gatherers. Archaeologists have unearthed ancient pits containing flint tools and weapons along with animal bones. Experts speculate these Neolithic people may have considered the area sacred because of the abundance of food. Gradually, beginning around 7500 bc, a culture- shaking change took place. The people inhabiting the area began slowly shifting from hunting and gathering to farming. Agriculture made it possible to produce food more reliably and feed larger groups of people. Farming also made large social projects possible. With sufficient food supplies, people there could spend less time hunting and had more time to work like Stonehenge. Sheep graze on the lush grass of Salisbury Sheep graze on the lush grass of Salisbury Plain near Stonehenge. Plain near Stonehenge.
Picture: Sheep graze on the lush grass of rolling Salisbury Plain, the hills stretching wide under open sky. Nearby, an ancient pit holds flint tools, weapons and animal bones left by hunter-gatherers. Further off, Neolithic people work small farm plots, tending crops and animals on land once wooded and full of wildlife.
7
A Project of 1,500 Years Archaeological evidence indicates the construction of Stonehenge began around 3000 bc. Amazingly, generations of people continued to work on it for more than a thousand years. Experts view the transport and placement of the giant stones as one of the great engineering feats of early human history. Archaeologists believe the builders brought the sarsen stones from distant quarries located about 32 to 48 kilometers (20–30 mi.) away. How were these giant stones hauled so far in a time before wheels and wagons? That question has led to far-fetched theories about giants, magic, and even space aliens. Archaeologists have focused on more realistic options. Teams of researchers have conducted experiments, trying to figure out how Stone Age technology could have been used to move the sarsen stones. Researchers have loaded replica stones of similar weights onto wooden sledges. They then slid these sled-like platforms along wooden rails greased with animal fat, pulled by dozens of volunteers. These trials proved it possible to move the giant stones to the Stonehenge site, and this has become a leading theory of how it was done.
Picture: Dozens of Neolithic volunteers strain on ropes, hauling an enormous sarsen stone on a wooden sledge. The sledge slides along wooden rails greased with animal fat, carrying the giant block across open ground towards the Stonehenge site, a feat of early engineering repeated again and again over centuries.
8
For many years, the bluestones presented a greater mystery since no source for them is found anywhere near Stonehenge. Eventually, investigations led researchers to a quarry in Wales—more than 241 kilometers (150 mi.) to the north. For some unknown reason, the creators of Stonehenge believed the heavy bluestones were important enough to transport staggering distances. Some experts speculate these stones may have been loaded onto rafts that floated along the ocean coast to bring them nearer to the Stonehenge site. These volunteers conducted an experiment in London in May 2016 to see if people could pull a heavy block of stone, such as the ones used at Stonehenge, without the use of machinery.
Picture: Workers cut heavy bluestones from a quarry in Wales, far to the north of Stonehenge, then load them onto rafts floating along the coast. In London in May 2016, a crowd of volunteers pulls a heavy block of stone with ropes and no machinery, testing whether people could move such weights.
9
Raising the challenge. One a pit under the base of each long slab of stone. Then, could have been Some researchers believe the builders beneath the top of Stonehenge raised stones by erecting wooden platforms beneath them. end, raising it little by little. Once the top was high enough, the bottom then dropped into the pit, and finally, people used ropes to pull it into vertical position. Placing the lintels on top of the trilithons may have required the most planning and expertise. One possibility is that the builders constructed ramps of dirt and wood that reached to the tops of each pair of standing stones. The lintel might then have been mounted on a sledge, where teams of workers would have used ropes to carefully slide it up the ramp and into place.
Picture: Builders raise a long standing stone by levering it up on wooden platforms, sliding it over the edge of a pit until the base drops in, then hauling it upright with ropes. Others drag a lintel on a sledge up a ramp of dirt and wood towards the waiting tops of two standing stones.
10
Who were these master builders who developed such innovative engineering techniques? It is nearly impossible to know what languages they spoke or which religions they practiced. They did not use writing and left behind few examples of their art. All modern understanding of them must be gleaned from archaeological evidence. Testing of human skeletons and animal bones found near the site offer fascinating clues about their origins. Some of the people who built Stonehenge seem to have moved there from Wales and other neighboring lands. Archaeologists excavate a quarry in Pembrokeshire, Wales, where Stonehenge’s bluestones possibly originated.
Picture: Archaeologists excavate a quarry in Pembrokeshire, Wales, the possible source of Stonehenge's bluestones, kneeling among cut stone faces and rubble. Close by, human skeletons and animal bones lie in the earth where they were found, the few clues left by builders who never wrote and left little art.
11
However, recent discoveries indicate that a few migrated from as far away as mainland Europe. The grand scale of Stonehenge suggests different groups banded together and shared their knowledge, skills, and muscle to create it. The Amesbury Archer In 2002, archaeologists discovered a skeleton near Amesbury, a town about 3.2 kilometers (2 mi.) from Stonehenge. He died about 4,300 years ago, during the time Stonehenge was still being built. He must have been important because his grave contained gold and stone wrist guards commonly worn by archers at that time. By testing chemicals in his teeth, researchers that he grew up in mainland Europe, near where Germany and Switzerland are today. For some yet unknown reason, this man migrated more than 1,126 kilometers (700 mi.). He died far from Earrings, rings, pots, and a knife are some Earrings, rings, pots, and a knife are some his homeland and was of the many objects found surrounding the of the many objects found surrounding the buried with honor. Amesbury Archer’s skeleton. Amesbury Archer’s skeleton.
Picture: The Amesbury Archer's skeleton lies in his grave near Stonehenge, gold ornaments and stone wrist guards beside him, with earrings, rings, pots and a knife scattered around the bones. He was buried with honour about 4,300 years ago, a man who grew up in mainland Europe, far from home.
12
Pondering the Purpose of Stonehenge As mysterious as Stonehenge is, clues about its purpose exist in its design. It does not seem to have ever served any practical purpose, like a shelter or market. For this reason, experts are convinced Stonehenge was an open-air temple, a place people visited for spiritual reasons. Researchers believe its religious and ceremonial purposes changed over the course of its long history. Stonehenge likely began as a burial site. Archaeologists have unearthed as many as 240 making it the England. Many of the bodies were and jewelry. A Neolithic man’s skeleton found at Stonehenge, along with his reconstructed face, are on display at the visitor center near Stonehenge.
Picture: A Neolithic man's skeleton lies in the earth at Stonehenge, one of as many as 240 burials found there, with grave goods and jewellery around the bones. In the visitor centre nearby, his skull and a reconstructed face are on display, showing a man whose people came to this open-air temple.
13
Another popular theory about Stonehenge involves astronomy, since some of the giant stones align with the movements of the sun. This is particularly apparent during the summer and winter solstices. The summer solstice, on about June 21 each year, marks the longest day of the year. At dawn that morning, the sun’s rays reach directly to the monument’s center. The winter solstice, which occurs about December 22, marks the shortest day of the year. At sunset, the sun shines directly between the largest pair of sarsen stones. In a sense, the arrangement of the monument’s stones functions like a giant clock marking the passing of the seasons. For a farming culture, this was vital information for planning when to plant crops. In recent years, another theory has emerged about the purpose of Stonehenge. Some experts believe it might have been a huge community project to unite people in the area. Evidence suggests that tribes from far and wide journeyed to Stonehenge and many settled there permanently. Perhaps they came to trade or band together for protection, or they could have been drawn by an abundance of food in the region.
Picture: At dawn on about June 21, the longest day, the rising sun's rays shine straight into the centre of Stonehenge between the standing stones. At sunset on about December 22, the shortest day, the sun drops directly between the largest pair of sarsen stones, marking the seasons like a giant clock.
14
The sun rises, shining through the stones of Stonehenge on June 21, 2014, the longest day of the year. Stonehenge Today Stonehenge is one of the most famous prehistoric landmarks in Europe. The Stonehenge the public sees today is not what people saw in 1900. By then, most of the giant stones had tilted or toppled. But in the last century, heritage groups have restored parts of the monument based on archaeological evidence. In 1958, cranes were used to lift some of the stones, and some of the standing stones were anchored in concrete. The restorers believed these restorations would give the public a better idea of the monument’s original appearance. Observers watch as the largest crane in England is used to place a lintel across two of Stonehenge’s standing stones in 1958.
Picture: The sun rises on 21 June 2014, shining through the stones of Stonehenge while onlookers gather on the grass. In 1958, observers watch as England's largest crane lifts a lintel and places it across two standing stones, part of the work restoring stones that had tilted or toppled.
15
Researchers continue to investigate Stonehenge and the surrounding area. Today, they use the latest technology to help them map what is hidden beneath their feet. This equipment includes magnetometers that detect magnetic fields, laser scanners that measure potential archaeological sites precisely, and ground- penetrating radar that can scan as deep as 9 meters (29.5 ft.) underground. Tourists can visit Stonehenge’s visitor center and interact with its exhibits. Replicas of Neolithic houses have been created using authentic materials and techniques, such as roof thatching, based on archaeological finds in the area. Volunteers demonstrate ancient rope weaving, pottery making, and grain grinding. More than nine hundred thousand people visit Stonehenge a year, including two children who try to pull a sarsen stone (left). Visitors go inside reconstructed Neolithic huts like those in which the builders of Stonehenge might have lived (right).
Picture: Researchers walk the site pulling a magnetometer and scanning the ground with radar equipment that maps what lies hidden nine metres below. Two children tug at a sarsen stone. Nearby, visitors step inside reconstructed Neolithic huts with thatched roofs while volunteers weave rope, shape pottery and grind grain.
16
The Story of Stonehenge Continues Stonehenge is an amazing landmark of an ancient time and place. Visitors marvel at the ingenuity of those who designed the monument to align with the sun’s movements. Impressed by its massive stones, they can picture hundreds of people working together to transport them to the site. They may wonder at the kind of teamwork and muscle it must have required to raise the heavy stones and link them together. For centuries, Stonehenge served as a central sacred site for the people who lived in the lands around it. The United Nations Educational, Scientific and Cultural Organization (UNESCO) named Stonehenge a World Heritage Site worthy and protection. Today, it stirs the people all around the world. Stonehenge is not the only stone circle in England. A larger stone circle has been found in Avebury, 40 kilometers (25 mi.) north of Stonehenge.
Picture: Visitors stand before the massive stones of Stonehenge, marvelling at the monument and picturing hundreds of people working together to drag and raise them. Away to the north, a larger stone circle at Avebury spreads across the English countryside, another ancient ring from the same distant time.

Argentina

13 pages · drawing · narrator: Documentary — A travel writer or places narrator would tell this out loud, giving a portrait of Argentina.
1
A gaucho rides his horse.
Picture: A gaucho rides his horse in an open outdoor setting. The rider sits on the horse’s back and holds the reins. The horse carries him forward. The gaucho and the horse are the main subject of the picture.
2
Argentina PARAGUAY BOLIVIA BRAZIL CHILE Buenos Aires PACIFIC URUGUAY OCEAN ARGENTINA ATLANTIC OCEAN Where Is It? Argentina is the second-largest country on the continent of South America and the eighth-largest country in the world. Its land covers 2,736,690 square kilometers (1,056,642 sq. mi.) and lies between the Andes Mountains, the longest mountain chain on Earth, and the Atlantic Ocean. The countries of Brazil, Uruguay, Paraguay, Bolivia, and Chile border Argentina. Twenty-three provinces make up Argentina. Each one has an independent government with its own laws and constitution. However, every province must follow and answer to the laws of Argentina’s national government.
Picture: A map shows Argentina within South America, between the Andes Mountains and the Atlantic Ocean. The country’s long shape is visible, along with the neighboring countries of Brazil, Uruguay, Paraguay, Bolivia, and Chile. Buenos Aires appears within Argentina.
3
The capital and largest city in Argentina is Buenos Aires, whose name means “fair or good winds.” Its population is more than fifteen million people. The city boasts the widest street in the world. People named Avenida 9 de Julio (July 9th Avenue) for the date of Argentina’s independence from the country of Spain. Buenos Aires is one of the largest ports in South America. The city is the center of many economic activities, including the nation’s banking, food processing, and automobile industries. Known for the friendliness of its residents, Buenos Aires is a multicultural city and home to diverse groups of people. It has one of the largest middle classes in Latin America. Many of the people who live and work there enjoy city life and all it has to offer. Buenos Aires looks quite a bit like a European city due to the traditions brought there by many of its residents. The obelisk on Avenida 9 de Julio was built in 1936. It honors the four hundredth birthday of Buenos Aires.
Picture: Buenos Aires is shown as a large, busy city. The wide Avenida 9 de Julio runs through it, with a tall obelisk standing on the avenue. European-style buildings line the streets, and people live and work in the multicultural city.
4
Argentina has a strong agricultural history. Farmers harvest wheat (left) and grapes (below). People More than forty-three million people live in Argentina, with more than 90 percent of the population living in its four largest cities. Argentina has the third-largest economy in Latin America, after Brazil and Mexico. Argentina is strong in agriculture and rich with natural resources such as silver, gold, oil, and natural gas. Its exports include wine and beef as well as lemons, grapes, peanuts, tea, and wheat. Most Argentines work in service jobs, but work is also available in the country’s automobile, textile, energy, communications, and appliance industries. Argentina also offers employment in the film industry, with dozens of motion pictures produced there every year.
Picture: Farmers work in fields, harvesting wheat and grapes. Golden wheat is gathered in one area, while bunches of grapes grow on vines in another. The scene represents Argentina’s strong agricultural history and the crops that are part of its exports.
5
The Roman Catholic faith is Argentina’s official religion. However, the country guarantees freedom of religion, and people also practice the Muslim, Jewish, Protestant, and Mormon religions. Like many countries, Argentina can be considered a “melting pot” because diverse peoples came together from all over the world to form a new nation. Argentina has always welcomed new immigrants. Today, the majority of Argentines have European roots. Argentina’s official language is Spanish. However, since most Argentines trace their roots back to Europe, some people also speak other languages, including Italian, German, French, and English. Visitors will notice European architecture in Villa General Belgrano, a district in the province of Córdoba.
Picture: People from diverse backgrounds stand together in a town with European-style buildings. A church and other places of worship suggest the country’s mix of faiths. The scene reflects Argentina as a melting pot of immigrants and languages from around the world.
6
Argentina’s Five Regions Land Shaped like a long PACIFIC OCEAN glaciers, vast lakes, and seashores. Five major regions make varied land. The ATLANTIC OCEAN Andes Mountains form a great wall between Chile and Argentina. The Pampas region is a large area of flat, fertile land where people grow many crops. Patagonia—the largest region and home to the oil and gas industries— is known for its ecotourism. The Littoral region, in the north, has jungles and swamps, while the Gran Chaco region is a lowland in the northwest. The countries of Brazil and Paraguay meet Argentina in an area called the Triple Frontier, which is formed by the Iguazú and Paraná Rivers. Here, the Iguazú River tumbles over horseshoe- shaped falls that extend 2.7 kilometers (1.7 mi.).
Picture: A map-like view shows Argentina’s long shape and its five major regions: the Andes Mountains, the flat Pampas, Patagonia, the northern Littoral, and the Gran Chaco. Rivers meet at the Triple Frontier, and the Iguazú River pours over horseshoe-shaped falls.
7
Iguazú Falls are taller and wider than most of the waterfalls in the world. They formed millions of years ago when a volcanic eruption left a huge crack in the earth. The name of the falls comes from a language of native people in the region and means “big water.” Iguazú Falls are one of the seven natural wonders of the world. With its many habitats, Argentina is alive with different kinds of birds and mammals. Toucans, Andean condors, penguins, pumas, tapirs, howler monkeys, and jaguars are all residents. The towering Andes Mountains, stretching the full length of western South America, are one of Earth’s great natural features. The Andes range is about 7,242 kilometers (4,500 mi.) long and runs through seven countries. Its highest peak— Mount Aconcagua, in the Mount Aconcagua—is 6,962 Andes, is the highest mountain meters (22,841 ft.) tall. peak in the Americas.
Picture: Iguazú Falls tumble over a wide, horseshoe-shaped cliff in a setting with many habitats. Birds and mammals such as toucans, condors, penguins, pumas, tapirs, howler monkeys, and jaguars live nearby. In the distance, the long Andes range rises, with Mount Aconcagua as its highest peak.
8
History Argentina has had a long and troubled history. Native peoples have lived here for thousands of years, but many died from disease and warfare following the Spanish invasion in the 1500s. After Argentina declared its independence from Spain in 1816, Spanish rule was finally banished in the country. The Argentine Republic was founded in 1853, and Buenos Aires became its official capital in 1880. Some people call the years from 1880 to 1914 the “Golden Age” of Argentina. Railroads expanded, sheep farming and cattle ranching became very important, and roads, buildings, and parks were built. However, with the Great Depression in 1929, Argentina’s economy struggled and military dictators rose to power. Dictatorships ruled from 1929 until 1943, when another Juan Perón, helped A painting shows the congress that declared Argentina free from Spain government. in 1816.
Picture: A historical painting shows the congress that declared Argentina free from Spain in 1816. Men gather in a formal assembly hall. The scene represents a turning point in Argentina’s long and troubled history before the republic was founded.
9
In 1946, Juan Perón was elected president. Perón’s popularity increased as he introduced reforms such as health services and retirement money for the majority of Argentines. Although he and his wife, Eva, introduced these Juan and Eva Perón good things to the working classes, Perón also ignored human rights and acted like a dictator. His popularity weakened during his second term as president, and he fled Argentina for Spain in 1955. However, Perón returned to Argentina in 1973. He ran for president once again and won a decisive victory. He died the following year. While the Peróns have critics, today many Argentines remember them as good leaders who helped the working classes and modernized Argentina. During the decades following Perón’s rule, Argentina experienced more military takeovers and economic turmoil. However, since 1983, democracy has taken hold, the economy has grown, and millions of new jobs have been created. Even with its rocky past, Argentina seems to have a promising future.
Picture: Juan Perón and Eva Perón are shown together as public figures. Juan appears as president, and Eva stands beside him. Supporters from the working classes look on. The scene recalls their reforms and the strong feelings many Argentines have about their legacy.
10
Celebrations Modern Argentina comes alive with its yearly festivals. Carnival, which is often celebrated in February or March, gives people a chance to dress up as clowns, animals, and cartoon characters. During Carnival, Argentines build huge, colorful floats that parade down the street, with musicians, dancers, and onlookers enjoying the event. People also enjoy wheat, tango, and gaucho festivals. The Gaucho Festival of San Antonio de Areco is a popular event that takes place every November. Gauchos show their horseback-riding skills during a ten-day celebration that includes bronco riding, parades, and dancing. Another important celebration in Argentina is the national day of independence on July 9. Families and friends spend time together to honor the events of 1816. They celebrate with traditional food and parades. Gauchos: South American Cowboys Long ago, gauchos roamed the Pampas herding cattle. Today, gauchos symbolize the spirit of the Argentine people. Gauchos are proud of their horses. It has been said that “a gaucho without a horse is a man without feet.”
Picture: During Carnival, people dressed as clowns, animals, and cartoon characters join musicians and dancers. Huge, colorful floats parade down the street while onlookers watch. The celebration fills the scene with movement, costumes, and festive energy.
11
Food As in other areas of Argentine culture, Spanish, French, and Italian immigrants to Argentina have brought their own tastes to what citizens eat. Today, foods from all these cultures are popular in Argentina. Since meat is widely plentiful, it is not unusual for people to eat beef twice a day. Steak and other cuts of meat are always available and are often grilled on an open pit or barbecue. Served nearly everywhere, empanadas are tasty pastries that can be filled with meat, vegetables, cheese, or fruit. Italian foods such as ravioli, pasta, and pizza are also popular. Argentine Tango Besides food, The complicated movements of a dance called the tango tell the story of everything from love to political unrest. Every year in August, people Argentines is a tea come to Argentina from all over the called yerba maté. world to take part in a tango festival. This drink is made from the dried leaves of the yerba maté plant. Argentines say it gives them energy and helps their minds stay alert.
Picture: A table shows Argentine foods: grilled steak and other cuts of meat on an open barbecue, tasty empanadas filled with meat, vegetables, cheese, or fruit, and Italian dishes such as ravioli, pasta, and pizza. A serving of yerba maté tea stands nearby.
12
Fountains decorate the area in front of the national government buildings of Argentina in Buenos Aires. Conclusion Solidly shaped by its European roots, Argentina has much to offer, including its beautiful land as well as its population, which has expanded to include people from China, South Korea, and other Asian nations. Argentines are committed to democracy and feel that their country will continue to prosper. A forward-thinking country, Argentina sets strict standards to protect the environment, encourages women to take part in government, and believes in equal rights for all people. Although political unrest has been a big part of the country’s past, its citizens—along with the rest of the world— hold great hope for its future.
Picture: Fountains decorate the area in front of Argentina’s national government buildings in Buenos Aires. The building stands behind the water, and the city setting is calm and orderly. The scene connects the government buildings with the country’s hopes for the future.
13
