Endurance on the Ice

16 pages · drawing · narrator: British man — A historian would tell this out loud, because it recounts a real past expedition and how its people survived.
1
The crew of Endurance take a group photograph next to the stuck ship.
Picture: A group of men in heavy polar clothing and fur hats stand close together on the ice beside the Endurance, their stout wooden ship trapped and tilted in the frozen Antarctic pack. They face forward, posed as a crew, with flat ice and pale sky around them.
2
Lost in the Antarctic The Imperial Trans-Antarctic Expedition was the brainchild of Sir Ernest Shackleton. The plan was to land Shackleton and his team on the Antarctic coast, where they would travel by dogsled and attempt the first crossing of the continent. The mission belonged to a British tradition of exploration that had helped map and document much of the globe. Antarctica remained one of the least-known regions in the world, its harsh conditions requiring extensive supplies, careful planning, and great courage. Shackleton had the courage and was no stranger to the Antarctic. He had made a name for himself during two earlier expeditions there. He was a tough but fair leader who never asked anyone to do what he would not do himself, and his men respected him for it. The Heroic Age of Polar Exploration By the late eighteen hundreds, Western explorers had set foot on nearly every patch of land on the globe. The polar regions, though, still held many mysteries that attracted intrepid adventurers. Teams that included Ernest Shackleton had twice attempted to reach the South Pole, once in 1902, then in 1909. Each time, they were forced back. Finally, in 1911, Norwegian explorer Roald Amundsen and his team became the first to stand on the globe’s southernmost point. Only weeks after Amundsen’s achievement, British Robert Falcon Scott also reached the South Pole. However, Scott and his four companions died of starvation and cold on the return journey.
Picture: Sir Ernest Shackleton stands on the Antarctic coast in heavy polar clothing, beside a loaded dogsled and stacked expedition supplies. His team prepares for the planned first crossing of the continent, while the vast, harsh ice stretches around them under a pale sky.
3
The crew of the Trans-Antarctic Expedition pose for a photograph on board Endurance. Shackleton had personally chosen Endurance, because he was impressed by the ship’s stout hull, which was designed to withstand and navigate heavy ice. He had also assembled a hardy and experienced crew. Captain Frank Worsley would prove an expert navigator in the most trying of circumstances. Frank Wild, Shackleton’s good friend, served as second-in-command. Other members of the team were likewise skilled and resourceful—essential qualities in a place as unpredictable as Antarctica. In August 1914, Endurance sailed from Great Britain. Four months later, on December 5, 1914, the ship left its last port on South Georgia Island in the far southern waters of the Atlantic Ocean. Just two days after leaving, Endurance encountered pack ice, which surrounded much of the continent. The ship plowed forward, using its engines and strong prow to bust through.
Picture: The crew of the Endurance pose together on the ship’s deck, wearing thick wool and fur clothing. The sturdy wooden ship with its strong hull sits in southern waters near South Georgia, surrounded by the first broken slabs of pack ice as it pushes forward.
4
But on January 18, 1915, ice completely enclosed the ship. It was about 160 kilometers (100 mi.) from the intended drop-off point, but its destination might as well have been on the Moon. The expedition team could not possibly reach the continent from the spot where they were stuck. In addition, unusual storm patterns caused the ice to remain locked around the ship even when it should have been breaking up. Miles and miles of excess ice accumulated between the crew and any possible escape. By February, the men knew there was nothing to do but wait for conditions to change. On board Endurance, the twenty-eight men remained comfortable and in good spirits. It was a strong ship with a thick hull. They worked, read, listened to records on a phonograph, and organized talent shows inside the ship’s warm belly. They had plenty of food and other supplies to last for many months. The crew set their hopes on November, when the ice usually broke up. The crew play soccer on the ice to pass the time while the trapped Endurance looms in the distance.
Picture: Twenty-eight men in heavy sweaters and boots play soccer on a flat stretch of frozen ice, kicking a ball while the trapped Endurance looms behind them, its wooden hull locked in the surrounding pack. The men smile and move briskly to pass the time.
5
Frank Wild surveys the wreckage of Endurance. Endurance did not last that long. In September, pressure ice closed in on the ship. Pressure ice consists of massive ice blocks driven by wind, currents, and storms. It started to squeeze Endurance like a nutcracker. The men on board despaired as the ship gradually tipped to one side. Its hull slowly bent, then cracked and split. The men compared the sounds it made to the moans and wails of a dying animal. In late October, the inevitable moment came. Shackleton gave the order to abandon ship. The crew salvaged a variety of useful supplies. Each man was allowed one set of clothing, plus his parka, two pairs of boots, and two pairs of mittens to protect against deadly subzero temperatures.
Picture: Frank Wild stands among broken pressure ice, surveying the Endurance as the ship tips and cracks, its hull split by massive ice blocks. Nearby, crew members salvage clothing, parkas, boots, and mittens from the wreck, their faces grim in the subzero Antarctic cold.
6
The men also moved the three lifeboats from the ship to the relative safety of the ice. These boats were their only chance of ever getting home. To survive the trip, however, they would need more provisions. Shackleton felt tremendous responsibility to save his men. He planned to lead them across the ice on foot to an emergency cache of supplies for shipwrecked sailors. They set out on November 1 but didn’t get far. Steep ridges of ice two stories tall crisscrossed the landscape. The going was exhausting, dangerous, and finally impossible. Shackleton called off the march after only 3 kilometers (2 mi.). They set up camp on the biggest, most stable ice floe they could reach. In the distance, they could still see the skeleton of their wrecked ship. On November 21, they watched Endurance disappear beneath the ice. The men retrieved more supplies from their first camp; then Shackleton ordered another march in late December. Once again, they made little progress. They picked another large ice floe and pitched their tents, calling the site Patience Camp. They now knew they had no choice but to wait for the floating ice pack to convey them to a site where they could launch their boats. From there, they would try to sail to whatever land they could reach.
Picture: Men drag three lifeboats from the wrecked Endurance onto a stable ice floe, then haul supplies across a landscape cut by steep two-story ice ridges. Tents stand at Patience Camp, and in the distance the ship’s skeleton sinks beneath the ice.
7
The men set up camp on an ice floe after moving supplies off the doomed ship. Patience on Ice Life at Patience Camp was harsh and unforgiving. Besides the extreme cold and frequent storms, the men had to contend with soggy gear and clothing. Most of the men slept crammed into five tents. Baths and clean clothes were out of the question. Despite these conditions, the men knew how to bear hardship. They entertained themselves by playing cards and sharing the few books they had. One sailor who had crafted a simple banjo led the rest in song. Food was always a favorite topic of discussion. Even though they were far from civilization, the men rarely went hungry. They ate two meals a day, almost always a calorie-packed stew called “hoosh.” It was made of crushed biscuits, water, a mixture of dried meat and fat called pemmican, and occasionally fresh seal meat and blubber.
Picture: At Patience Camp, men crowd into five small tents on the ice floe, their gear soggy and clothes unwashed. Some play cards or share books, one sailor strums a homemade banjo and leads singing, while another stirs a pot of thick hoosh stew.
8
Hunting teams had good luck supplementing food stores with sea lions and penguins. Blubber—fat from the seals—also served as good cooking fuel. Meanwhile, the ice under them was moving with the ocean currents. Captain Worsley took regular sightings using his sextant, a navigational tool that measured the position of stars or the Sun to calculate location. Patience Camp was slowly drifting toward the edge of the pack ice, where they hoped to launch the boats. In March 1916, the men began sensing the rise and fall of the ice beneath their feet. By Worsley’s calculations, they had traveled more than 3,200 kilometers (2,000 mi.) since Endurance had become trapped. They noticed the ice becoming more fragile and prepared the boats in case they had to launch at a moment’s notice. Finally, on April 9, the long-awaited moment came, and they launched the boats into an opening in the ice. With some crewmates working the oars and others at the bow shoving aside blocks of ice, they drove for open water. Once they were in more open seas, they turned their boats toward Elephant Island. It was uninhabited and far from help, but any solid land improved their chances of survival.
Picture: Hunting teams return with sea lions and penguins, and men butcher the catch near the tents. Others launch the three lifeboats into an opening in the pack ice, rowing hard while crewmates at the bows shove aside floating blocks with oars, steering toward open water and Elephant Island.
9
To Elephant Island Against the nightmare of the stormy Antarctic seas, Shackleton and his men pitted their muscles, minds, and willpower. With boats that lacked engines, their only propulsion came from two sets of oars and small sails. During the long, dark nights, the three crews worked to stay in sight of each other amid the monstrous waves. What followed was a voyage of agony and tenacity. As storms battered the lifeboats with hurricane-force winds and slashing sleet and rain, waves of freezing water crashed over the sides. Frost and ice crystallized on everything and everyone. The crews suffered from seasickness and frostbite. When crew members finished their turns at the oars, they huddled together for warmth. Sleep was all but impossible.
Picture: Three small lifeboats toss across monstrous stormy waves under a dark sky, powered only by oars and tiny sails. Men row with straining muscles, freezing spray and ice coating their clothes, while others huddle together for warmth, soaked and seasick, struggling to keep the boats in sight.
10
Worsley’s navigational skills in these conditions proved miraculous. On the seventh day, the high peaks of Elephant Island loomed dead ahead. The exhausted crew—men who had barely slept for seven days—finally made camp on a stretch of narrow, rocky beach. On April 16, 1916, for the first time in 497 days, the crew of Endurance set foot on land. However, they were still far from anywhere they wanted to be. There was no reason any ship would sail near enough to discover them. Someone had to venture on for help. Little more than a week after reaching Elephant Island, Shackleton, Worsley, and four others set out again in the James Caird, one of the lifeboats. Shackleton left Frank Wild in charge of the twenty- two men who waited behind. All their hopes were pinned on Shackleton’s dash for help. No one dared think what they would do if he failed. Ernest Shackleton Frank Wild Frank Worsley.
Picture: Exhausted men camp on a narrow rocky beach below the high peaks of Elephant Island, their first land in 497 days. Near the shore, Shackleton, Worsley, and four crew members prepare the James Caird, while Frank Wild remains with the twenty-two men waiting behind.
11
Across the Deadliest Sea The James Caird was modified for the voyage. The men had raised the sides and stretched a canvas deck across the top for protection against the tumultuous water. They had loaded supplies and hundreds of pounds of rocks into the lifeboat’s bottom. The extra weight would help steady the boat in rough weather. Their destination was South Georgia Island, the same place they had sailed from nearly a year and a half earlier. To get there, they would have to cross 1,287 kilometers (800 mi.) of the most treacherous seas in the world. Shackleton, Worsley, and four other crew members launch the James Caird and set out for South Georgia Island.
Picture: On a rocky Antarctic shore, men load the James Caird with supplies and hundreds of pounds of rocks. The lifeboat has raised sides and a stretched canvas deck for protection. Six crew members push off and launch toward South Georgia Island across 1,287 kilometers of treacherous sea.
12
The 6.7-meter (22 ft.) craft and its six-person crew had to battle for every mile. The waves were massive, and the weather battered them from every direction. The man working the rudder—usually Shackleton or Worsley—was often the only person with his head above deck so he could see. The others huddled down below, drenched from the steady wash of waves. Teams took turns managing the sails or working a hand pump to keep water from flooding the boat. Whoever was off duty attempted to steal moments of sleep near the prow. Several times, sleet and freezing rain layered the boat with ice, adding dangerous weight. Men risked their lives to crawl out onto the deck and chip it loose. Day after day, night after night, the boat plowed on. One night, Shackleton was at the rudder. Glancing back, he saw a line of white in the darkness above him. At first, he thought it was a break in the clouds. Then he watched in terror as the line rose higher. It was the crest of a gigantic rogue wave. He cried out, warning the men to hold on. It came down on them like a giant’s fist, flinging the boat forward. In an instant, the boat was half-filled with water. The men bailed with anything they could get their hands on. Ultimately, their desperate efforts saved their lives.
Picture: The small James Caird climbs a gigantic rogue wave, its canvas deck awash. One man grips the rudder above deck while others huddle below, drenched, or work a hand pump. Sleet layers the boat with ice, and the crew bails desperately as freezing water floods in.
13
After seventeen brutal days at sea, they finally caught a glimpse of South Georgia Island—a most welcome sight. Again, Worsley had worked navigational magic in seeking out a small bit of land in the middle of boundless ocean. Beyond exhausted, the six men stumbled ashore. Unfortunately, they had landed on the western coast, but the whaling station was on the island’s other side. Sailing around the island was ruled too dangerous. Someone would have to hike 35 kilometers (22 mi.) for help, a feat easier said than done. Steep mountain peaks iced with glaciers divided South Georgia Island. No one had ever climbed them before. After some days of rest, three men set out— Shackleton, Worsley, and Tom Crean. They headed up the slopes and into the glaciers. Rock walls blocked their way and forced them to double back. The ice fields were so steep, they had to chop steps into the ice. Slowly, they slogged their way up until they reached the ridge of peaks that divided the island. They had just clambered over the ridge when Shackleton realized a different danger was closing in on them. They were on a high slope, night was falling, and the temperature was rapidly dropping.
Picture: Shackleton, Worsley, and Tom Crean climb steep glaciers on South Georgia Island, chopping steps into the ice as they go. Rock walls force them to double back, and they struggle upward toward the ridge of peaks as night falls and the temperature drops sharply around them.
14
They needed to get to a lower elevation or they would freeze to death—and they needed to do it fast. With little debate, the three men sat down on the snowy slope and linked arms and legs. They pushed off and sped down the mountain on their backs. In less than two minutes, they dropped more than 305 meters (1,000 ft.). As they slid to a halt, the men burst into laughter, then rose and continued their trek. The next afternoon, on May 20, 1916, a trio of filthy bearded men in torn and tattered clothing walked into the whaling station on South Georgia Island and were led to the station’s manager. They had been hiking for thirty-six hours straight.
Picture: Three bearded men in torn, filthy clothing sit linked arm-in-arm and leg-to-leg on a steep snowy slope, then push off and slide downhill on their backs, snow spraying around them. High icy mountains rise behind as they drop more than 305 meters in under two minutes.
15
The twenty-two crew members wave to their rescuers from Elephant Island. They were marooned there for over four months. All Safe The twenty-two men back on Elephant Island remained cautiously hopeful. The earliest they expected rescue was in late May 1916, if Shackleton had been successful. May came and went, then June and July. As the days of August ticked off, the men feared the worst. Still, each day they tramped up to an overlook and scanned the sea. Each day they slouched back down, disappointed. Then on August 30, sharp eyes spotted a pillar of smoke on the horizon. The men hurried to get a fire burning to signal their location. The Chilean ship Yelcho steamed into view. The men watched as a small boat was lowered over the side, and they soon recognized a familiar figure in the prow. It was Shackleton, calling out to ask how they were. Some of the men wept with relief. Shackleton had led three earlier attempts to reach his stranded men. Each time, the ships were forced back by dangerous ice or mechanical problems. Determined to rescue his crew, Shackleton pressed on. Now, finally, he had made it back to Elephant Island.
Picture: Twenty-two bearded men on Elephant Island wave and shout from a rocky beach beside a signal fire. Offshore, the Chilean ship Yelcho steams into view, lowering a small boat. Shackleton stands in its prow, calling out to the crew, and some men weep with relief.
16
Twenty-eight men had sailed for Antarctica more than two years before. Now all twenty-eight were going home. Against tremendous odds— a wrecked ship, months in subzero temperatures on treacherous ice, and the crossing of deadly seas in small lifeboats—every single man who set out on Endurance of the greatest survival stories ever lived or told. Endurance Expedition: From Stranded to Rescued Endurance Expedition: From Stranded to Rescued 2.
Picture: All twenty-eight men stand together on the deck of a rescue ship heading home, their faces tired but relieved. Behind them lie the frozen Antarctic, Elephant Island, and the wrecked Endurance. They have survived the ice, the lifeboat voyages, and the deadliest sea.

American Farms: Past, Present, and Future

20 pages · drawing · narrator: Professional woman — A historian would tell this out loud, explaining how American farms and farm life have changed over time.
1
A farmer teaches another generation about greenhouse crops.
Picture: A farmer stands inside a greenhouse, showing a younger person how crops grow. The farmer gestures toward the plants while the younger learner watches, linking one generation to the next in a working farm setting.
2
A Nebraska homesteader family stands in their celery field in 1904. Picture a Farm In 1901, John and Magreta Hoefer started a family farm on 160 acres (0.65 sq km) near Elgin, Nebraska. Their five girls and two boys would wake early before school to do their chores: open the henhouse, milk the cows, slop the hogs, and feed the horses. After school—especially in spring, summer, and fall—everyone hitched up their suspenders to tend to the gardens and fields. Almost all the work was done by hand and muscle, with horses to haul the wagons. It was a big effort to grow and raise enough food for their sizable family, enough feed for their livestock, and some surplus to sell.
Picture: A Nebraska homesteader family stands among celery plants in 1904. Parents and seven children gather in the field on their family farm, with cows, horses, hogs, and a henhouse nearby as part of the morning and after-school chores.
3
Today, John and Magreta would be astonished to see what their small farm grew into. Their children, grandchildren, and great- and great- great grandchildren expanded their holdings and now manage more than 1,000 acres (4 sq km) of land split between seven Hoefer-owned farms. Today’s Hoefers do not need the whole clan to work the soil and tend to fields and livestock. They have a fleet of modern machinery and equipment, including big tractors, combines, and automated irrigation systems. Computers help them monitor and manage operations. They raise cattle and grow corn and soybeans as cash crops, along with specialty crops like popcorn and organic vegetables. American agriculture—and the U.S. economy overall—has changed dramatically in the last two hundred years. These shifts fundamentally altered what people do for a living and how they feed their families. Where once most everyone lived on a farm, now only a small percentage of Americans work the land. Yet American farms feed the nation, ship tons of food overseas, and are an important part of a global system of agribusiness. American farmers have shown a remarkable ability to adapt to change. They will need similar resilience to manage changes on the horizon.
Picture: Modern Hoefer descendants manage a large farm with big tractors, combines, and automated irrigation systems. Computers monitor the operation while cattle graze and fields of corn, soybeans, popcorn, and organic vegetables stretch across seven family-owned farms in the present day.
4
A farmer guides a plow in Montana around 1900. Past: The American Tradition of Farming The United States was once a nation of farmers. In 1790, 90 percent of American families lived and worked on small farms. Their goal was to grow and store enough food to get through the winter, which required careful planning. A bad growing year due to drought, flood, or a disease of crops or livestock was not just a disappointment; it could mean hunger and financial disaster. Draft animals such as horses, mules, and oxen provided the power in front of a wagon or ground- breaking plow. Working the land also required constant human labor. On smaller family farms, entire families, from grandparents to young children, tackled the dawn-to-dusk chores that kept home and farm functioning. Others lived on larger farms, called plantations, enslaving people to work their land and produce commercial crops such as cotton or tobacco. Even with hard work, success or failure was often in the hands of unpredictable elements like weather and farm product prices.
Picture: A farmer in Montana around 1900 guides a ground-breaking plow pulled by draft animals across open land. The scene shows the heavy human and animal labor that once powered American family farms from dawn to dusk.
5
By the early 1900s, Americans were in the middle of a big social shift. The Industrial Revolution, which had begun in the late 1700s, was accelerating, with more and more people leaving rural farms for city factories. Interestingly, a revolution in farming helped make this possible. By 1900, the system of slavery had been abolished and family farmers made up only 38 percent of the country’s labor force. Yet even as that percentage shrank, farmers were able to grow enough food to feed millions in cities as well as their own communities. Three developments ramped up farmers’ productivity and allowed them to meet the growing nation’s food needs. The first, mechanization, was the direct result of technological development. Powered farm machinery allowed one person to do the work of dozens. Second, improved farming practices made it possible to grow more crops on less land. Farmers utilized improved seeds, chemical fertilizers, weed killers, and irrigation technology. Third, modern transportation systems allowed farmers to distribute their products over great distances. No longer were they limited to selling crops locally or regionally. Instead, they could truck them to trains or ships to reach national and even international markets.
Picture: Farmers use powered machinery, improved seeds, fertilizers, weed killers, and irrigation to grow more crops. In the background, people leave rural farms for city factories, while trucks, trains, and ships carry produce to national and international markets.
6
The development of American agriculture was also aided by the nation’s expansion. Starting in the 1860s, the United States colonized the Great Plains. This wide region of prairies and other grasslands stretches westward from the Mississippi River to the Rocky Mountains. The land was mostly treeless with rich soil, making it ideal for farmers and ranchers to cultivate and exploit. It was easier to work than the rocky and forested areas back east. The Great Plains are still referred to as the “breadbasket” of the United States, even the world. This region is capable of producing vast amounts of food. Present: Family Farms as Big Business The trend toward fewer farmers producing more food has continued. Today, farmers and farm laborers make up only about 2 percent of American workers. Yet American farms produce more agricultural products than ever before. Farm size helps tell the story of American agriculture today. According to the United States Department of Agriculture (USDA), midsize and large-scale farms make up only 9 percent of American farms. However, they account for a remarkable 65.2 percent—nearly two-thirds— of food production.
Picture: Wide, treeless prairies stretch westward from the Mississippi River toward the Rocky Mountains. Farmers and ranchers work the rich soil of the Great Plains, a region that can produce vast amounts of food and is known as a breadbasket.
7
Despite the considerable size of some of these American farms, almost all of them continue to be owned and operated by families. Less than 2 percent of farms in the United States are “nonfamily farms” owned by outside corporations. Farm Size vs. Farm Production U.S. Farm Size U.S. Food Production by Farm Size 2.9% 1.3% 6.1% 10.6% 24.2% 42.4% 22.8% 89.7% KEY: Small Midsize Large-scale Nonfamily Family *The USDA defines a farm’s size by its income. Small farms generate $350,000 or less each year; midsize farms earn $350,000 to $999,000 annually; and large-scale farms bring in at least $1 million each year.
Picture: A visual comparison groups small, midsize, and large-scale farms beside family and nonfamily farms. It shows that most farms are small and family-owned, while a small share of large-scale farms produces nearly two-thirds of American food.
8
However, most family farms are incorporated, which can offer important business advantages. Owners may pay less in taxes, be better able to secure bank loans, and have more protections if they have financial problems or the owners retire or die. Frahm Farmland Inc. is a prime example of a large-scale farm. Lon Frahm is its chief executive officer (CEO)—the top boss. His family has farmed in Kansas for six generations. When he took over the family farm in 1986, it had 5,000 acres (20 sq km) of cropland. Today, his team of about ten employees farms almost 27,000 acres (109 sq km). They grow corn, soybeans, wheat, and sorghum, a protein-rich grain used to feed livestock. Like many American farms, Frahm Farmland has greatly expanded its production in order to be successful and profitable.
Picture: Lon Frahm, chief executive of Frahm Farmland Inc., stands with his team of about ten employees on a large Kansas farm. His family operation has grown from 5,000 acres in 1986 to almost 27,000 acres, growing corn, soybeans, wheat, and sorghum.
9
Corn, Cornier, Very Corny! Bushels of corn per acre More Food in the Field Productivity is a driving principle of every for-profit business, including agriculture. Through the years, American farms have continued to become more productive. Since the 1960s, the total number of U.S. farms has stayed fairly steady at about 2.1 million. The amount of farmland has stayed about the same as well. Yet during that period, American farmers have more than doubled their output of agricultural products.
Picture: Rows of corn fill a field, representing the bushels of corn grown per acre. American farms have more than doubled their output since the 1960s even though the total number of farms and the amount of farmland have stayed fairly steady.
10
Migrant farm workers sort and pack red bell peppers in Southern California. Keeping production costs low and profits high also helps explain why many large American farms employ immigrants as farm workers. Whether undocumented or legal guest workers, they are often paid wages far below those of American workers. To optimize efficiency, many big farms also practice monoculture—the intensive growing of one farm product, be it a crop or a breed of livestock. This approach allows such farms to invest only in the equipment and facilities that serve their purposes. Owners also develop expertise in the production of that product.
Picture: Migrant farm workers sort and pack red bell peppers in Southern California. They work with piles of bright peppers, showing the intensive labor behind large farms that keep production costs low and profits high through immigrant workers and monoculture.
11
The risks of this scheme include catastrophic weather events and disease outbreaks that can wipe out that single crop. The lack of diversity degrades the soil and requires heavy use of fertilizers. It also leaves farmers vulnerable to significant drops in the market price of whatever they grow or raise. The Question of Factory Farms Some big livestock farms are designed to produce as many animals for slaughter as fast as possible. They are sometimes called CAFOs, short for “concentrated animal feeding operations,” or “factory farms.” Animals at these facilities are often held in small indoor pens with no natural light and little room to move about. Dairy cattle, hogs, and chickens are the livestock most often managed this way. Criticism of CAFOs focuses on three main issues: (1) these operations produce tons of animal manure, which harms the environment; (2) the animals are often raised and slaughtered in cruel conditions; and (3) the crowded conditions can result in disease outbreaks in livestock. To counter this, animals are given high levels of antibiotics, but this can result in germs resistant to antibiotics. Since people take in those same antibiotics when they eat the livestock, it can ultimately Hens are crowded together put their health at risk. on a factory farm.
Picture: Hens are crowded together inside a factory farm with little room to move and no natural light. The scene shows the confined indoor pens used for dairy cattle, hogs, and chickens in concentrated animal feeding operations.
12
To offset their risks, about a third of American farmers sign production contracts with giant agribusinesses. The farmers agree to raise produce or plant seeds supplied by these powerful corporations. In return, the companies agree to pay the farmers a set price for the products. However, these contracts give the companies a great deal of control over pricing. They can also require farmers to make expensive upgrades to their facilities at the farmers’ expense. Farmers may even be put out of business for speaking publicly about how the company treats them. Farmers must weigh the costs and benefits of such production contracts. On one hand, these agreements offer some guaranteed financial backing for farmers’ hard work and investments. On the other, farmers can be left at the mercy of giant agribusiness. Even large-scale farms have limited power to negotiate terms with these corporations, which increasingly control not only agriculture but the food industry as a whole. How does the efficiency of big farms and food production affect the average American? In general, it means lower prices. The less it costs to produce milk, grow corn and wheat, or raise chickens and pigs, the more those savings can be passed on to consumers.
Picture: A farmer weighs a production contract offered by a giant agribusiness. On one side are guaranteed payments and financial backing; on the other are company control over pricing, expensive facility upgrades, and limited power to negotiate, affecting the cost of food for consumers.
13
How Much Do Americans Spend on Food? Percentage of income Year The cost of food has gone up a lot since 1930. Yet by 1997, the percentage of their income that Americans spent on food had dropped to less than half of what it was early in that century. Future: Global and Local Farming Farmers often try to see the future. They invest in crops or livestock for the promise of future financial returns. They calculate whether investing in more acreage or a high-tech tractor will benefit their productivity. They check the weather forecast as well as the clouds to see whether they should cut the hay now or wait for the thunderstorm to pass. Planning ahead is a fundamental part of a farmer’s life. So what does the future hold for American farms and farmers? That depends on where you look. Two significant trends are shaping the future of agriculture in the United States—one global, the other local.
Picture: A farmer pauses in a hayfield, looking from dark clouds to a weather forecast, deciding whether to cut hay now or wait for a thunderstorm. The scene shows farmers planning ahead by calculating investments and watching weather and market conditions.
14
Agriculture is now a global business, and most economists agree that globalization is shaping farming practices—in the United States and elsewhere. Globalization refers to the trade and movement of products and money between international businesses and banks. In many ways, it is already affecting what people buy and eat as well as how they make a living. For example, soybeans harvested in Nebraska or Iowa may be shipped to China, bringing Chinese capital, or money, to the United States. Avocados picked in Mexico are trucked to food distributors in the United States in exchange for U.S. dollars. Globalization offers expanded economic opportunities for American growers to sell more of their products overseas. At the same time, it creates risks that events in other countries will affect American farms. If the United States and China get into a trade disagreement, for instance, China can limit imports of American soybeans and corn. As part of globalization, giant agribusinesses are also reaching across borders and oceans like never before. They are buying or renting the resources they want and need wherever those resources may be.
Picture: Soybeans harvested in Nebraska or Iowa are loaded for shipment to China, while avocados picked in Mexico travel by truck to U.S. food distributors. The scene shows global trade in farm products and the risks when countries disagree over imports.
15
In 2013, the Chinese conglomerate Shuanghui International purchased Smithfield Foods Inc. for $4.7 billion. Based in Virginia, Smithfield is the biggest pork processor in the United States. After the sale, China controlled more than four hundred farms, thirty-three processing plants, and one out of every four hogs in the United States, according to the USDA. Such international business deals involving food processors and distributors are likely to grow more common as globalization extends its reach. Even as large-scale farms go global, some smaller American farms are going in a very different direction—more local. Small farms make up almost 90 percent of American farms. However, they produce only 24.2 percent of farm products. About half of small-farm owners report that their main income comes from off-farm jobs. Some small farms are actually considered “hobby farms” that owners run more for enjoyment than profit. Still, the “local food” movement is turning up in more communities in the United States. A growing number of these farmers are shifting.
Picture: A large pork processor and its many farms and hogs are shown under international ownership after a Chinese conglomerate purchased Smithfield Foods. In contrast, a small farm owner works off the farm and sells locally, reflecting the local food movement.
16
A farmer’s market in Los Angeles offers local, organic vegetables. away from the goal of mass production. Instead, they sell what they grow at nearby farmer’s markets and restaurants. Many also choose to farm using more traditional methods, limiting or eliminating the use of chemical fertilizers, pesticides, and herbicides. Whether large-scale or small, though, future farm owners in the United States will need to grapple with environmental challenges. Agricultural experts worry that current farming practices are unsustainable because they are depleting valuable resources. Research indicates that farms in some areas are pumping too much water from underground wells and draining their sources. Some large-scale animal operations are generating massive amounts of pollution that are poisoning waterways and harming air quality. Overuse of fertilizers, pesticides, and herbicides is damaging valuable soil.
Picture: A farmer's market in Los Angeles offers local, organic vegetables. Farmers sell what they grow at nearby markets and restaurants, using traditional methods that limit chemical fertilizers, pesticides, and herbicides, while experts worry about water depletion, pollution, and damaged soil.
17
