Picture: A child plants vegetables in dark, nutrient-rich soil while many cows are milked at once in a milking parlor. Around them, robotic milking machines, drones, driverless tractors, and moisture sensors show technology on large farms, alongside no-till fields and rotated crops.
18
Climate change causes flooding (left) and desertification (right). Both hurt farmers. A bigger environmental issue looms on the horizon as well: climate change. Average temperatures are rising around the globe, and weather patterns are changing. Areas that once could plan on regular rainfall are seeing less precipitation, while other regions are receiving much more. Worldwide, climate researchers are observing an acceleration of cropland loss and growing deserts, especially in arid regions. Climate change is a contributing culprit. In the United States, studies indicate that climate change could greatly reduce farm productivity. By the year 2050, yields of corn and soybeans could drop by up to 25 percent in the Midwest. Such a decrease could have a significant effect on food supplies in North America and around the world.
Picture: A split landscape shows farmland damaged by climate change: flooding on one side and desertification on the other. Both hurt farmers, as rising temperatures change rainfall, accelerate cropland loss, and could sharply reduce corn and soybean yields in the Midwest.
19
By its nature, agriculture cannot adapt quickly to big, rapid changes. Farming, like most businesses, benefits from predictable conditions that allow for careful planning. However, the future is likely to bring bigger, more extraordinary variations in both economics and climatic patterns than American farmers have had to deal with in a long time—or maybe ever. A Changing Way of Life The business of American agriculture has been expanding since the nation’s founding. Farms have grown in size and sophistication. Their productivity has supplied a growing nation and helped feed a world population that has doubled since 1970 to almost eight billion people. Meanwhile, American farmers go about their daily work—planting seeds, growing crops, raising livestock, and producing the food we eat. Longtime farmers consider farming as much a lifestyle as it is a way to make a living. They find satisfaction in caring for their land, crops, and animals. That ethic has changed little in the last one hundred years. However, a generational shift is underway in terms of who owns and operates American farms. The average American farmer is fifty-eight years.
Picture: An older farmer, about fifty-eight, works in a field while younger people choose other work and walk away from farming. The scene shows the generational shift in who owns and operates American farms, even as farmers keep planting, growing crops, raising livestock, and producing food.
20
Farmers will still be farming decades from now, adapting to whatever changes come. What their farms will look like at the end of this century—and who will be driving the tractors, picking the corn, and milking the cows—is more difficult to say. A farmer teaches his grandchild to drive a tractor. But will his grandchild still drive a tractor when they grow up? old—nearing the age when many American workers retire. Fewer young people are choosing to farm. Even those with farming in their blood are walking away from the hard work, long hours, and unpredictable outcomes and income.
Picture: A farmer teaches his grandchild to drive a tractor across the family farm. The scene looks ahead to future decades, when farmers will still be farming and adapting to change, though it is uncertain who will drive tractors, pick corn, and milk cows.

Ivory or Elephants

20 pages · photo · narrator: Documentary — A nature-documentary narrator would tell this aloud, describing elephants and explaining the ivory trade from outside the story.
1
This hippo is carved from ivory. Dubious Treasures Near the end of your tour of several Asian countries, you still haven’t found the perfect souvenir to bring home. You find yourself wandering along busy rows of tents, carts, and tables at an outdoor market. A table at the far end of the market catches your eye as you try to escape the noise. The table is lined with sculptures, mostly of animals. You ask about a couple of small white ones labeled “ivory.” They’re expensive, but they’re beautiful and small enough to carry on your long flight home. The vendor talks about the artist who made the ivory sculptures. He stresses how well-made the pieces are and how they will last forever. The vendor leaves out a few key details, however. He doesn’t tell you what ivory is or how it was obtained. He doesn’t talk about the illegal wildlife trade, which is crucial to the availability of the sculptures, or the brutal slaying of elephants.
Picture: A hippo carved from ivory rests among animal sculptures on a table at the far end of a busy outdoor market. Tents, carts, and tables line the noisy rows. A vendor stands by the table, speaking with a traveler who studies the small white pieces and thinks about a souvenir for the long flight home.
2
What Is Ivory? Ivory is a hard cream-colored substance that makes up the tusks of elephants. Less commonly, the term is also used for the tusks of walruses, hippos, narwhals (a type of whale), and wild boars. Tusks are very long teeth that extend outside the mouth when it is closed. In most tusked mammals, the tusks are the upper canine teeth, but in elephants they’re the front teeth, or incisors. Tusks are made of a hard, bony tissue known as dentine. Enamel, a harder material on the tips of elephant tusks, wears away over time. Tusks grow continuously throughout an elephant’s lifetime. African elephant skull with tusks.
Picture: A large African elephant skull with curved tusks stands against a plain background. The long front teeth extend outside the jaw, showing the hard, cream-colored ivory. The scene also includes a walrus, a hippo, a narwhal, and a wild boar, each with tusks.
3
Modern elephants are either African or Asian; African elephants are the larger of the two. African elephants are further divided into forest and savanna species. Males and females of both species grow tusks. The tusks of savanna elephants, the larger species, can grow to over 2.1 meters (7 ft.) in length. Among Asian elephants, some males have prominent tusks, while other males, as well as most females, have thin, short tusks that often break off. Tusks are a structural adaptation that give elephants a survival advantage. Elephants use their tusks to collect and transport food, to dig for roots and minerals, for display, and as weapons. Some elephants use them to dig water holes during the dry season. Tusks also offer protection for an elephant’s trunk. Tusks are deeply embedded in an elephant’s upper jaw. The only way to obtain an elephant’s tusks is by killing the elephant. Elephants use their tusks for many tasks, including digging (left) and lifting (right).
Picture: Modern African and Asian elephants stand in savanna and forest habitats. A large African savanna elephant shows long tusks over two meters in length, while an Asian elephant has shorter tusks. One elephant digs for roots with its tusks; another lifts food, and a third stands guard with its trunk protected behind its tusks.
4
About Elephants Elephants are Earth’s largest land animals, measuring up to 3 meters (10 ft.) at the shoulder. Male African elephants weigh as much as 6 metric tons (13,200 lb.). Although elephants look vastly different from us with their lumbering bodies and huge ears and trunks, they are extremely intelligent. Their brains are as large in proportion to their body—and as complex—as our own. They are among Earth’s most intelligent animals. Elephant Ancestry Elephants are the only living members of a family of mammals that also includes mastodons and mammoths, which are both extinct. Their closest living relatives are hyraxes— small, furred herbivores that look somewhat similar to prairie dogs. Other close relatives include manatees and dugongs, which look as though they would be more closely related to dolphins. Nevertheless, elephants, hyraxes, manatees, and dugongs all share a common ancestor. Hyraxes (left) weigh up to 5 kilograms (11 lb.). Dugongs (right) weigh up to 300 kilograms (661 lb.). Elephants weigh up to 11,000 kilograms (24,251 lb.)!
Picture: Elephants, Earth's largest land animals, stand with a hyrax and a dugong. A male African elephant shows its huge body, large ears, and trunk, measuring up to three meters at the shoulder. A small furred hyrax rests on rock, and a dugong swims in the sea, showing relatives that share a common ancestor. Extinct mastodons and mammoths appear nearby.
5
An elephant matriarch leads her family to a water hole in South Africa. Elephants are skillful problem solvers and tool users, often working together to invent new ways to do things. They live as long as some humans do—into their seventies—and have strong social bonds. Elephants maintain multigenerational family groups of females for life, guided by a wise older female known as a matriarch. She is responsible for keeping her family together, making sure they’re safe, and leading them to food and water. In times of drought, matriarchs have been known to find water holes they haven’t visited in decades. Elephants have complex emotions and have been observed laughing and playing. They also show great compassion toward each other. When an elephant feels distress, others stand next to her, soothe her with their trunks, and make soft, comforting sounds. These behaviors are notably similar to what humans do when a friend is in distress, except that we use a hand instead of a trunk. Elephants also display grief when a family member dies.
Picture: An elephant matriarch leads her family to a water hole in South Africa. Several elephants gather at the water, with the older female at the front. Nearby, elephants touch a distressed family member with their trunks and make soft, comforting sounds, showing strong social bonds and compassion within the multigenerational herd.
6
Two young African elephants mourn the death of an adult female. Beyond their exceptional intelligence, elephants are vital members of the animal kingdom in another way as well. Scientists consider them keystone species—species on which many other species depend. Elephants play an essential role in keeping their ecosystems in balance. They dig water holes that also provide water for other animals. They clear land of trees and bushes, which creates more habitat for grassland species. The trails they create help keep fire from spreading and also assist with water drainage. In addition, some seeds must pass through an elephant’s digestive system in order to begin to grow, and their dung enriches depleted soil enough for people to grow crops. In short, elephants are essential to the ecosystems in which they live.
Picture: Two young African elephants stand beside the body of an adult female, mourning her death. Around them, elephants dig water holes, clear trees and bushes, and create trails through the grassland. Seeds and dung enrich the soil, showing how elephants help many other species in their ecosystem.
7
An elephant hunter poses with his trophies in Sudan around 1925. The Ivory Trade Ivory has been prized by people for thousands of years and has been carved into art objects as well as jewelry and many other items. Although Asian elephants have been killed for their tusks— as well as their meat and skin—the ivory trade has a much greater effect on African elephants for two reasons: their tusks are larger, and both males and females grow them, so the supply is greater. Tracing the impact of the ivory trade on elephant numbers over the past two hundred years is sobering. In 1800, an estimated twenty- six million elephants lived in Africa. In the early 1900s, killing African elephants on safari was popular among wealthy Westerners. There was also a high demand for items made from ivory, including piano keys, pool balls, combs, and brush handles. According to National Geographic.
Picture: An elephant hunter poses with his trophies in Sudan around 1925. The hunter stands beside the dead elephants and their tusks. Nearby, carved ivory art objects, jewelry, piano keys, pool balls, combs, and brush handles show the many uses of ivory that have been prized for thousands of years.
8
In the 1970s and 1980s, the demand for ivory increased sharply again. An estimated one hundred thousand African elephants were being poached, or killed illegally, each year. Elephants were in serious trouble, and something had to be done quickly to stop their decline. Word Wise Poaching generally refers to the illegal act of hunting, capturing, or killing wild animals in violation of any law meant to protect wildlife. It includes illegally trapping or shooting animals on both public and private lands. Poaching most often takes place for commercial profit or for sport. Rangers and game wardens enforce anti-poaching laws. Rangers and vets attempt to remove a poacher’s snare from an elephant’s feet in Kenya. by 1913 the United States’ consumption of ivory had reached about 181 metric tons (200 tn.) each year, and the number of African elephants had dropped to about ten million.
Picture: Rangers and veterinarians work with an African elephant in Kenya, attempting to remove a poacher's snare from its feet. The elephant stands while the team carefully treats the injured leg. In the background, other rangers patrol the area to enforce anti-poaching laws and protect elephants from illegal hunting.
9
An official who works for Zimbabwe’s national parks inspects the country’s ivory stockpile in 2016. In 1989, CITES (SY-teez), an international agreement that governs trade in endangered plants and animals, banned the trade of ivory. The ban dramatically increased public awareness about the elephant crisis. Prices dropped steeply as the demand for ivory plummeted, and elephant numbers started to recover. The global ivory ban had the potential to lead to the long-term recovery of African elephants. However, the increase in elephant numbers over the next ten years prompted four African countries—Namibia, Botswana, Zimbabwe, and South Africa—to ask CITES to allow them to sell their stockpiles of ivory. They claimed that elephant populations in their countries were either stable or on the rise. Furthermore, they stated that.
Picture: An official who works for Zimbabwe's national parks inspects the country's ivory stockpile in 2016. The official stands among stacked tusks and carved ivory pieces, checking the stored supply. The scene shows the large stockpile that countries held after the international ivory trade ban and later debated selling.
10
CITES approved a sale in 1999 but only to countries with strict guidelines preventing illegal trade. Japan was the only country that qualified. Another sale took place in 2008, this time with both Japan and China buying ivory. The second sale had the unintended effect of increasing the demand for ivory. It also gave the impression that ivory trading could again be legal at some point in the future. As a result, illegally killing elephants for their tusks skyrocketed, and many countries started stockpiling ivory in hopes of making huge profits down the road. Some countries have burned their stockpiles of ivory. Doing so ensures that the ivory will never be traded illegally. the sales would only involve tusks obtained from natural deaths or from earlier culling—the killing of elephants to control their numbers in areas with limited space.
Picture: Countries burn their stockpiles of ivory, with tall flames and smoke rising from piles of tusks. Officials stand nearby as the ivory is destroyed so it can never be traded illegally. In the background, other stockpiles remain stacked, recalling the sales approved by CITES in 1999 and 2008.
11
The crisis has been escalating ever since and has reached a tipping point: more African elephants are now being killed by poachers than are being born each year. Unless that trend is quickly reversed, it spells doom for elephants, not in some vague, distant future, but rather within our lifetimes. According to WildlifeDirect, fewer than 400,000 African elephants were alive in 2018, and 35,000 were being killed each year to satisfy the demand for ivory in Asian countries. If that rate continues, the ongoing slaughter of African elephants will guarantee their extinction by 2030. African Elephant Population elephant numbers (millions) 1.3 2 1 600,000 352,000 0 1800 1913 1979 1989 2016.
Picture: An African elephant stands beside a visual record of its falling population, shrinking from millions in 1800 to fewer than 400,000 in 2018. The steep decline continues as poachers kill 35,000 elephants each year, suggesting extinction by 2030 if the trend is not reversed.
12
A caretaker walks with an orphaned elephant calf in Kenya. In addition to the looming threat of extinction, other tragic situations are also linked to the ivory trade. One is the trauma experienced by surviving elephants that witness killings and lose family members. As a result of that trauma, some elephants become aggressive and antisocial. Young males seem to be especially affected by the loss of mothers and older males. Orphaned adolescents have been known to go on killing sprees that target other young elephants, other species such as rhinoceroses, or humans. In addition, young females that have witnessed killings sometimes grow up lacking mothering skills and reject their babies. Given elephants’ high intelligence and strong social bonds, scientists aren’t surprised by their response to trauma.
Picture: A caretaker walks with an orphaned elephant calf in Kenya. The young elephant follows close to the caretaker along a path, showing the care given to a calf that lost its family. Nearby, surviving elephants stand apart, reflecting the trauma, aggression, and social problems that can follow the loss of mothers and older family members.
13
Another concern is the grave danger faced by rangers who protect elephants. Long ago, the term poachers generally referred to poor villagers who illegally killed a few animals to feed their families, and rangers often arrested them without incident. Today’s poachers are completely different, using state-of-the-art weapons in carefully planned attacks. In April 2016, an attack by heavily armed elephant poachers in Garamba National Park in the Democratic Republic of the Congo killed three rangers and seriously injured the park’s manager. Similar attacks have taken place in other parts of Africa. More than 450 rangers were killed by wildlife poachers between 2009 and 2017. In July 2018, an international ranger organization reported that 107 rangers had been killed during the previous year. Another consequence of poaching is a significant increase in elephants born without tusks. During a civil war (1977–1992) in the African country of Mozambique, roughly 92 percent of the 2,500 or so elephants in Gorongosa National Park were slaughtered for both ivory (to buy weapons) and meat (to feed the fighters), and most of the 200 or so surviving elephants lacked tusks. Under normal conditions, only around.
Picture: Heavily armed poachers attack rangers in Garamba National Park in the Democratic Republic of the Congo. Rangers stand on guard in the park with weapons ready, protecting elephants from carefully planned attacks. In another area, rangers patrol against poachers who use state-of-the-art weapons, and more than 450 rangers have been killed between 2009 and 2017.
14
African elephants are born without tusks, but since the war, more than 50 percent have been born tuskless. The occurrence of tuskless elephants has also increased in other African countries where poaching is common. The field of genetics helps shed light on this shift. Tuskless elephants are more likely to survive in areas where ivory poaching is common. Therefore, they are more likely to pass the trait of tusklessness down to their offspring. Normally it would take many, many generations for such a shift to take place. However, the high rate of poaching is causing the shift to occur much faster than normal by giving tuskless elephants an enormous survival advantage. At the same time, the situation raises the question of how elephants survive without tusks. Elephant scientists are just beginning to explore that question. Tuskless elephants tend to eat more grasses and fewer woody plants than tusked elephants. No one yet knows how that shift will affect their health and long-term survival.
Picture: Tuskless elephants feed in an African savanna, eating more grasses and fewer woody plants than tusked elephants. A family group shows several females without tusks, a trait that has become common where poaching is heavy. Scientists study how tusklessness spreads through genetics and what it means for the elephants' health and long-term survival.
15
Other Threats As formidable a threat as poaching is to elephants, they are also at risk for other reasons. • Habitat loss – Long ago, elephants roamed great distances in search of food and water, following ancient migration paths. As humans have encroached more and more on their territory by building homes, farms, roads, railroads, and pipelines, elephants have lost a great deal of their range, and what remains is increasingly fragmented. Habitat loss and fragmentation not only prevent access to resources but also separate groups of elephants. This can limit their choice of mates and reduce genetic diversity, which is important to their survival. • Conflict with humans – Encroaching human populations also result in more elephant-human conflicts. Wherever elephants are not in protected areas, they compete with humans for food, water, and space. In some areas, elephants enter villages looking for food and damage crops and property. Farmers and villagers often kill or injure trying to chase them away, and elephants sometimes kill or Kenyans watch an elephant across the fence that protects their crops. injure people as well. • Culling – Conflicts with humans can become enough of a problem that wildlife managers kill a large number of elephants in order to control their numbers. Like poaching, culling often causes trauma in the elephants left alive.
Picture: Kenyans watch an elephant across a fence that protects their crops. Beyond the fence, the elephant stands near a village and farmland, showing where elephants and people compete for food, water, and space. In the distance, roads, homes, and farms fragment the elephant's habitat, while wildlife managers consider culling to control elephant numbers.
16
Saving Elephants The effort to save elephants from extinction requires global action involving governments, organizations, and individuals. A key component is making sure that laws and regulations, both national and international, ban commercial trade in ivory. At the present time, CITES prohibits all commercial international trade in the ivory of both African and Asian elephants. A number of countries with significant ivory markets, including the United States, United Kingdom, China, and Singapore, either already prohibit commercial ivory trade or will enact bans in the near future. A significant step took place in December 2017 when China, once the largest ivory market in the world, banned all domestic ivory sales and closed its ivory-carving factories and shops. Other Asian countries that still buy and sell ivory are being pressured to follow suit. In addition to enacting bans, countries must also effectively enforce them. Carvings such as these are no longer sold in China because of the 2017 ivory ban.
Picture: Carvings made from ivory sit in a workshop or shop, no longer sold in China because of the 2017 ivory ban. Nearby, government officials and conservation workers discuss laws and regulations that prohibit commercial ivory trade. The scene shows countries enforcing bans and closing ivory-carving factories and shops to protect elephants.
17
Eliminating the demand for ivory is another crucial piece of the puzzle. As long as demand is high, illegal ivory trade will continue, even with laws in place. Conversely, greatly reduced demand will cause ivory trafficking —and therefore the slaughter of elephants—to no longer be profitable. Conservation groups work tirelessly to educate the public about the plight of elephants. This is especially true in Asia, where demand continues to be high. While working to reduce the demand for ivory, various conservation organizations have reported that many people don’t know that elephants must die for their tusks to be taken. Public awareness campaigns are correcting that misconception. People march in April 2019 to end poaching and the illegal wildlife trade in Kenya.
Picture: People march in April 2019 to end poaching and the illegal wildlife trade in Kenya. Marchers walk together through the streets, drawing attention to the plight of elephants. Conservation groups work to educate the public in Asia and elsewhere, correcting the misconception that elephants do not have to die for their tusks.
18
Elephant Heroes Many dedicated people are part of the effort to save elephants. Among them is Bryan Christy, an investigative journalist. Christy had a realistic fake tusk made that contained a GPS tracker. He planted the tusk in a region of Africa known for ivory poaching and tracked its path. Christy discovered that ivory poaching was being carried out by African terrorists— Christy’s research on ivory was featured groups that use or threaten in a 2015 issue of National Geographic. violence to try to achieve their goals—with the support of high-ranking people in government and the military. Conservation biologist Dr. Sam Wasser of the University of Washington in Seattle studies elephant DNA in an effort to combat poaching. For more than two decades, he has been using DNA from elephant dung to create a map of elephant genetics. The map allows him to identify where poached ivory is coming from and has helped law enforcement officials concentrate on those locations. Much of the illegal ivory from Africa travels to Asia through Hong Kong. In 2013, three Hong Kong schoolgirls—Nellie Shute, Christina Seigrist, and Lucy Skrine—formed a group to educate people about the connection between ivory and elephant deaths. They collected more than eighteen thousand signatures on a petition to urge Hong Kong’s government to destroy its huge stockpile of ivory, which had been seized from smugglers. The task, which was completed in 2016, was one of the largest known ivory destruction projects in the world.
Picture: Bryan Christy, an investigative journalist, holds a realistic fake tusk that contains a GPS tracker. He stands in a region of Africa known for ivory poaching, tracking the tusk's path. Nearby, Dr. Sam Wasser studies elephant DNA from dung, and three Hong Kong schoolgirls collect signatures on a petition to destroy a huge ivory stockpile.
19
Working with local communities is another part of the effort to save elephants. People who have had conflicts with elephants often have negative attitudes toward them. These people benefit from support to deal with the problems so that killing isn’t the only option. Conservation organizations educate local people about elephant habits and habitats, along with the danger of extinction from poaching and habitat loss. They provide information about how to live in harmony with elephants. For example, one organization teaches farmers to plant red pepper around their crops. This plant bothers the eyes and noses of elephants and causes them to leave the area. Conservation organizations also encourage people to consider elephants as a precious resource of their country—one they can take pride in that can also attract tourists and become a source of income. In addition, connect with young people and inspire them to help elephants survive. Schoolchildren visit an elephant orphanage in Kenya.
Picture: Schoolchildren visit an elephant orphanage in Kenya. They stand near young elephants and learn about elephant habits, habitats, and the danger of extinction. Nearby, a farmer plants red pepper around crops to keep elephants away, showing how conservation organizations help local communities live in harmony with elephants and see them as a source of pride and tourist income.
20
Three elephants in Botswana approach a water hole at sunset. Time Is Running Out Now imagine that outdoor Asian market again, with its beautiful ivory sculptures. People who don’t know where ivory comes from will keep buying it, and elephant numbers will continue to drop. The severe decline of elephant populations is a complex topic and also a controversial one. Disagreement exists about whether to ban all ivory trade or regulate it. Some people think the only way to save elephants is with a total ivory ban. Others believe that a total ban will increase the illegal ivory trade, drive ivory prices higher, and create more incentive for poachers to keep killing elephants. Will time run out for elephants while people struggle to find answers? The clock is ticking. For now, two things are certain: elephants are being slaughtered at a rate of about a hundred each day, and a world without elephants would be an impoverished place to live.
Picture: Three elephants in Botswana approach a water hole at sunset. The elephants walk together across the land toward the water, their bodies outlined by the low sun. The calm scene contrasts with the warning that elephants are being slaughtered at a rate of about a hundred each day and that a world without them would be impoverished.

Volcanoes

20 pages · drawing · narrator: Professor — A science explainer would tell this aloud, walking listeners through how volcanoes work and what they do.
1
This volcano shows signs of activity. The Eruption of Vesuvius Few people in the Roman city of Pompeii were alarmed by the earthquakes that began shaking the region in 79 ce. They had endured seismic activity in the past and had rebuilt their city whenever any significant damage occurred. Usually, though, the earthquakes were minor, and people had grown accustomed to them. The residents of Pompeii loved their beautiful city and would not leave just because the ground occasionally shook or a building fell down. No one connected the trembling ground with the plume of smoke that steadily hissed from nearby Mount Vesuvius. After all, people had lived in Pompeii for centuries without ever witnessing a volcanic eruption, and the slopes of Mount Vesuvius were teeming with life and were covered with trees, flowers, and grass. It had been a thousand years since the volcano had erupted, so most people assumed that it was dormant, or even extinct.
Picture: A smoking Mount Vesuvius rises near the Roman city of Pompeii. People go about their lives as the ground trembles, and buildings show old damage. The mountain's slopes are green with trees, flowers, and grass, while a thin plume of smoke hisses from the summit under a calm sky.
2
But everything changed on August 24 around one o’clock in the afternoon. Suddenly, a tremendous explosion shook the area around the mountain, sending molten rock, ash, and dust spewing from the summit of the volcano and darkening the sky. This dust, ash, and stone (collectively known as tephra ) rained down on the city, covering the ground as far away as 16 kilometers (10 mi.) from the mountain. By three o’clock, lava gushed from the mouth of the volcano at an alarming rate, destroying everything in its path. By six o’clock, the cloud of dust and ash had ascended to 32 kilometers (20 mi.) in height, and the swirling energy inside it generated lightning bolts. The ash continued to blanket the area, covering the ground to a depth of 1 meter (3.3 ft.) and causing buildings to collapse under the weight of the falling debris. Mount Vesuvius overlooks the excavated ruins of Pompeii.
Picture: Mount Vesuvius explodes with a tremendous blast, sending molten rock, ash, and dust from the summit and darkening the sky. Lava gushes from the volcano's mouth and races across the land. A towering ash cloud rises high, swirling with lightning as debris collapses buildings below.
3
People in the cities surrounding Mount Vesuvius tried to evacuate the area as the volcano demolished their homes and farms. They gathered their valuables and most portable treasures and hurried their families away from the disaster. Some people tried to traverse the thick layers of ash that covered the ground, but the ash was so deep and so hot that many people died. Others were struck and killed by stones falling from the dark sky, and some suffocated on the ash- and dust-clogged air. Around midnight, the crisis grew even worse. The massive cloud of ash, poisonous gases, glowing-hot dust, and smoke had become so dense and heavy that it could no longer remain airborne. The cloud collapsed into a pyroclastic flow, racing down the mountain at speeds of up to 500 kilometers per hour (310 mph). Glowing-hot lava bubbles up inside a volcano.
Picture: People gather valuables and hurry their families away from the eruption as ash covers the ground. Some try to cross deep, hot ash; falling stones and clogged air threaten them. Near midnight, a dense cloud collapses into a pyroclastic flow and races down the mountain, while glowing-hot lava bubbles inside the volcano.
4
A plaster cast of the body of an inhabitant of Pompeii who perished during the eruption of Mount Vesuvius Do You Know? Pompeii, a city not far from Mount Vesuvius, was completely buried by a surge cloud. Many perished under the surge cloud, but as their bodies decayed, they left air pockets in the hardened ash and mud that surrounded them. These air pockets preserved the exact shapes of the bodies they once contained. Archaeologists filled the holes with plaster to make casts showing how the people looked when they died. This was Mount Vesuvius’s first surge cloud, which within a few moments scorched and buried the people in the cities of Pompeii and Herculaneum as it swept over them like a wave. As more dust and ash billowed from the volcano, more surge clouds followed. When the eruption was over, two cities of the Roman Empire were completely buried and wiped off the face of the Earth. They would not be rediscovered for over a thousand years.
Picture: A plaster cast of a Pompeii inhabitant lies in hardened ash, preserving the shape of the body left in an air pocket. Archaeologists made such casts by filling those holes. Nearby, the buried cities of Pompeii and Herculaneum lie under layers left by a surge cloud.
5
What you just read is an account of a real event, but how did it happen, and what could cause such a catastrophic explosion? And if volcanoes are so dangerous, why do people choose to live near them? People and Volcanoes People live near volcanoes for a variety of reasons. They often find nutrient-rich soil for farming in these regions or wonderful hot springs where they can take relaxing baths. Still, people have learned over time to respect volcanoes. Long ago, people had no way to scientifically predict or understand volcanoes, but volcanoes were woven into their beliefs, traditions, and lore. For instance, in Indigenous Hawaiian culture, there are legends about the goddess Pele (PAY-lay), whose anger caused volcanoes to erupt. Some types of volcanic glass, such as Pele’s hair and Pele’s tears, are named in honor of this goddess. Pele’s tears are small drops of lava that cool and become solid while flying through the air.
Picture: Near a volcano, people farm nutrient-rich soil and relax in hot springs. In Indigenous Hawaiian tradition, the goddess Pele expresses anger through eruptions. Tiny drops of lava cool in the air as Pele's tears, while thin threads of volcanic glass form Pele's hair.
6
Devil’s Tower is an extinct volcano in Wyoming that holds deep spiritual significance for many Indigenous tribes of the Great Plains. The Lakota call it Bear Lodge, while the Kiowa call it Tree Rock. It is considered a sacred place of creation and renewal where pilgrims make offerings, pray, and perform ceremonies. In ancient Rome, people believed that the Roman god Vulcan worked in a blacksmith’s forge beneath Earth’s surface. They saw volcanoes as the chimneys of the underground forge where Vulcan fashioned lightning bolts for use by Jupiter, the king of the gods, and made weapons and armor for Mars, the god of war. The word volcano comes from the name Vulcan. Even today, volcanologists say that volcanoes smell like a blacksmith’s forge. In Japanese Shinto and Buddhist traditions, Mount Fuji holds great spiritual significance. It is considered a stairway to another world and revered as the spiritual home of Kono-hana, a Shinto deity. It has become Devil’s Tower, also known as Bear’s Lodge and Tree a sacred center for both Rock, is an extinct volcano pilgrimage and art in Japan. in Wyoming.
Picture: Devil's Tower rises as a sacred site where Indigenous pilgrims pray and make offerings. In Roman belief, Vulcan works a forge beneath the earth, and Mount Fuji stands as a Japanese spiritual center and stairway to another world.
7
While volcanoes remain spiritually and symbolically significant in many cultures, today scientists are also able to use tools and instruments to learn more about how they develop and function. Scientists can even predict when volcanoes will next erupt. Where and Why Volcanoes Form The first volcanoes appeared about 3.5 billion years ago, covering vast areas with hot lava that spewed from deep within Earth. Earth’s hard surface, or crust, is made of many continent-sized sheets of rock called tectonic plates. Some plates make up the continents themselves, while others make up the ocean floors. Just under the surface of the plates, the Earth is extremely hot (about 1,200° Celsius or 2,192° Fahrenheit)—so hot that rock melts into a liquid called magma. Sometimes, the its way up to the surface and seeps out through cracks in the crust, to form.
Picture: Long ago, lava covers vast areas of the young Earth. Beneath the planet's rocky crust, intense heat melts rock into magma. The magma sometimes rises toward the surface and seeps through cracks in the crust to form a volcano.
8
Plate Movement Volcanic Trench Oceanic crust Mantle Mantle When an ocean plate crashes into a continental plate, the ocean plate moves down, creating an active volcanic region. Scientists estimate that more than fifteen hundred different volcanoes have erupted in the last ten thousand years, and every year about thirty-five or forty volcanoes erupt around the world. Sometimes the only sign of volcanic activity is a small amount of smoke or steam emerging from a volcano. Some volcanoes are always erupting slowly, while others, like Vesuvius, can remain quiet for hundreds or even thousands of years between explosive eruptions. Volcanoes usually form at the edges of tectonic plates, which are slowly floating on the thick, liquid magma underneath them. But the various plates are moving in different directions, and as they move, they can crash into, pull away from, or grind past other plates. As the huge plates slide and rub against each other, their movements create large cracks in the crust where magma can break through to form volcanoes.
Picture: An ocean plate slides beneath a continental plate at a volcanic trench, while hot mantle rock lies below. Deep cracks open in the crust as huge plates shift, letting magma rise. A volcano sends out a small wisp of smoke or steam above the moving plates.
9
Pacific Ocean EQUATOR More than half the active volcanoes in the world occur along the Ring of Fire Ring of Fire. There are at least five hundred active volcanoes in the world, most of which are located near the edges of tectonic plates. The edge of the Pacific Ocean plate is a particularly dense volcanic region known as the Ring of Fire. Another notably active volcanic region runs along the Mid-Atlantic Ridge, which is a mountain chain in the Atlantic Ocean. Two plates are drifting apart in the Atlantic Ocean, allowing magma to seep up through the ocean floor, where it accumulates. Over time, the magma can build up and create mountains so tall that they emerge from the water. The country of Iceland sits atop the summits of large volcanoes in the Mid-Atlantic Ridge.
Picture: The Pacific Ocean's Ring of Fire curves around the ocean, holding many active volcanoes. In the Atlantic, two plates drift apart along the Mid-Atlantic Ridge, and magma seeps up through the ocean floor. Over time it builds mountains that rise from the water, with Iceland sitting atop their summits.
10
Other volcanoes are located in areas far away from the edges of the plates. For example, the islands of Hawai‘i are in the center of the Pacific Ocean plate. They are located above a “hot spot,” a place where hot magma sits very close to the surface of Earth’s crust. Sometimes the magma creates a new island, but the process is gradual, taking tens or even hundreds of thousands of years. In Yellowstone National Park, in the middle of the North American plate, an underground hot spot sits directly beneath underground lakes and waterways. The magma heats up the ground and the water in this area like a giant teapot, creating geysers and hot springs. You can visit Yellowstone in Wyoming and see water erupting from the ground at geysers such as Old Faithful. Hawai‘i Kauai hot spot Kauai Hawai‘i.
Picture: In the middle of the Pacific plate, Hawaiian islands rise above a hot spot where magma sits close to the crust. At Yellowstone, an underground hot spot heats water and ground, making geysers and hot springs erupt. Old Faithful shoots heated water into the air.
11
Different volcanoes erupt in different ways, depending on where and why the volcano forms. Some volcanoes erupt gently and gradually, while others suddenly explode with the force of many atomic bombs. Let’s examine different kinds of volcanoes and learn how they erupt. Composite Volcanoes Mount Vesuvius, which you read about earlier, is a composite volcano on the west coast of southern Italy. Composite volcanoes are explosive volcanoes—their eruptions can be extremely violent and destructive. Also called stratovolcanoes, they are the most common type of volcano. They are usually very large mountains with steep sides and evenly shaped peaks, often with a bowl- shaped crater at the top. The crater is a hollow area where the magma, hot gases, and ash come out. The impressive shape of a composite volcano.
Picture: A composite volcano rises as a large mountain with steep sides and an evenly shaped peak. A bowl-shaped crater opens at the top, where magma, hot gases, and ash escape. Mount Vesuvius stands on the west coast of southern Italy as an example of this explosive kind.
12
Mount St. Helens Mount St. Helens before 1980 eruption after 1980 eruption Do You Know? When Mount St. Helens, in Washington State, erupted on May 18, 1980, sixty-three people died and 311 square kilometers (193 sq mi.) of forest were flattened by hot winds that blew down the mountainside. Most composite volcanoes occur where the magma near the surface is viscous —thick and slow moving like syrup. Magma moves toward the surface in tubes called vents. When the magma is unusually thick, it can cool and harden inside the vent, plugging the vent and trapping the magma below. Because the heat and gases beneath the plugged vent have nowhere to go, the pressure slowly begins to increase. Eventually the pressure becomes so great that it reaches a critical threshold, and the volcano explodes. Sometimes the explosion is so violent that the whole mountain is destroyed in the blast. When Washington State’s Mount St. Helens erupted in 1980, the entire northern side of the mountain was obliterated.
Picture: Mount St. Helens rises in Washington State, with its northern side whole and then blasted away. Thick, slow magma hardens inside a vent and traps heat and gas below. Pressure builds until the volcano explodes, flattening a wide area of forest.
13
When composite volcanoes erupt, they send massive clouds of ash, dust, smoke, hot gas, and rock into the air. These clouds rise for many kilometers, turning the sky black and raining ash onto the ground. When a cloud of debris becomes too dense to remain in the air, it collapses in a violent surge cloud. Many composite volcanoes are very tall, and they are often capped with ice and snow. In an instant, an explosion can melt all the ice and snow on the top of the volcano, sending a flood of water, mud, and rock racing down the mountain at up to 100 kilometers per hour (62 mph). Such a mudslide triggered by volcanic activity is known as a lahar. Melting ice and snow sent a massive lahar racing down the side of this volcano.
Picture: A composite volcano blasts a towering cloud of ash, dust, smoke, hot gas, and rock into the sky. The cloud darkens the air and rains ash onto the ground. Melted ice and snow rush down the mountain as a fast lahar of water, mud, and rock.
14
Crater Lake is a well-known caldera in Oregon. This huge caldera was formed when the volcano collapsed in on itself. Do You Know? The Klamath people in southern Oregon have passed down a legend about the origin of Crater Lake. According to their tradition, the first coyote fell in love with a star and found a way to join her in the sky. When he fell back to Earth, his impact made a hole that became Crater Lake. Some volcanic ash clouds are so gigantic that the ash and dust can travel around the world, blocking out sunlight and cooling down the entire planet. After the 1815 eruption of Tambora, a volcano in Indonesia, people living as far away as North America experienced a cold, snowy summer. Sometimes an entire underground lake of magma can pour out of a composite volcano, leaving a huge empty space below. With nothing to support it, the surface collapses into the empty chamber, leaving a large bowl-shaped basin called a caldera. Calderas can measure as much as 100 kilometers (62 mi.) from side to side. Over time, they often fill with water, creating large lakes such as Crater Lake in Oregon.
Picture: Crater Lake fills a wide caldera in Oregon, a bowl-shaped basin left when a volcano collapsed into an empty magma chamber. A Klamath story tells of a coyote falling to Earth and making the hole. Over time, calderas often fill with water.
15
Composite volcanoes can destroy huge forests, bury entire cities, and even result in human deaths. These volcanoes are very dangerous because they often remain quiet for hundreds of years between eruptions. People forget that the volcano might erupt, and they build their homes dangerously close to the mountain. Shield Volcanoes You may have seen a video of bright orange lava flowing or spraying from the top of a volcano. The lava, which is very runny, flows smoothly and quickly, moving like water in a stream. The lava spreads out over the land before slowly hardening and building up. This type of lava comes from a shield volcano. Shield volcanoes usually have gentle, smooth mountain slopes in the shape of shields. Mauna Loa, a shield volcano in Hawai‘i.
Picture: Bright orange lava flows smoothly and quickly from a shield volcano, spreading like water in a stream before hardening. The mountain has gentle, smooth slopes shaped like a shield. Mauna Loa in Hawai'i rises as an example, while people build homes close to quiet composite volcanoes.
16
Olympus Mons, on the surface of Mars, is the largest known volcano in the solar system. It would cover the entire state of Arizona. Eruptions of shield volcanoes are usually gentle rather than explosive, although the lava flows can still destroy roads, homes, and forests. Sometimes a shield volcano contains hot gases or steam that sprays from the crater, creating a bright lava fountain. Since the paths of the lava flows are stable and predictable, scientists are often able to get very close to study them. Shield volcanoes can remain quietly active for long periods of time and grow to become extremely large in the process. Mauna Loa on Hawai‘i is the tallest volcano in the world, rising 9,170 meters (30,080 ft.) from its base on the seafloor, making it taller than Mount Everest. If Mauna Loa erupts again, it could grow even taller. However, even Mauna Loa seems tiny when compared to the largest known volcano in our solar system—Olympus Mons on Mars. This enormous Martian shield volcano stands 27 kilometers (17 mi.) tall.
Picture: Olympus Mons on Mars rises as the largest known volcano in the solar system, while Mauna Loa in Hawai'i towers from the seafloor. Shield volcanoes erupt gently, and their runny lava can still destroy roads, homes, and forests. Scientists study stable lava paths from close by.
17
Cinder Cones and Lava Domes Some volcanoes are not active long enough to form large mountains. Instead, they may just spray small bits of lava into the air for brief periods of time. The small lava chunks and bits of ash harden into lightweight black rocks called cinders. The cinders pile up around the vent into a cone-shaped hill with a crater at the top. Such hills are called cinder cones. Brand-new volcanoes that suddenly appear often form cinder cones. Other cinder cones pile up inside the craters or calderas of larger, older volcanoes. Most cinder cones erupt only once. Because cinder cones are made of loose rocks, they usually erode quickly in the wind and rain. Cinder cones at Mauna Kea volcano, Hawai‘i.
Picture: Small bits of lava and ash spray into the air and harden into lightweight black cinders. The cinders pile around a vent into a cone-shaped hill with a crater at the top. These cinder cones rise at Mauna Kea in Hawai'i and often erode quickly in wind and rain.
18
Lava dome atop Novarupta vent, Katmai National Park and Preserve, Alaska (above); a scientist monitoring a lava dome inside a crater Lava domes also result from small, brief eruptions, but the lava that forms these structures is a thick, pasty liquid that oozes from the vent and quickly hardens. Sometimes, more lava pushes up and expands through the center of the dome, cracking the outside. These domes often form in areas with other volcanic activity. They are often found in the craters and calderas of larger volcanoes. Like composite volcanoes, lava domes often explode violently.
Picture: A thick, pasty lava dome sits atop a vent in Katmai National Park and Preserve, Alaska. A scientist monitors another dome inside a crater. New lava pushes through the center and cracks the outside, and these domes often form in the craters of larger volcanoes.
19
Conclusion Volcanoes are the most spectacular evidence we have that Earth is a changing planet. Lava pouring from a volcanic vent creates new rock and new land, and volcanic ash makes extremely fertile soil that is useful for farming. In some places, people use the geothermal energy from volcanoes to run power plants and produce electricity. Some people even live inside volcanoes. Rabaul, Papua New Guinea, is a port city built close to an active volcano that erupts periodically. Volcanic mountains, including Mount Fuji in Japan, Mount Rainier in Washington, and Mauna Loa in Hawai‘i, are some of the most recognizable and beautiful mountains in the world. It is no wonder that humans often choose to live near volcanoes, but we be dangerous. Volcanoes are home to some of Earth’s most powerful forces. Beautiful Mount Fuji in Japan.
Picture: Mount Fuji rises as a beautiful volcanic mountain in Japan, joined by Mount Rainier and Mauna Loa as recognizable peaks. Lava creates new rock and land, volcanic ash makes fertile soil, and geothermal energy runs power plants. Rabaul in Papua New Guinea is a port city built near an active volcano.
20

Wildlife Rescue

20 pages · photo · narrator: Clear adult man — An explainer would tell this book aloud, describing how wildlife rehabilitators do their work and why it matters.
1
Tucson Wildlife Center.
Picture: A child sees a wild animal in trouble and alerts a trusted adult, who reaches for a phone book to find wildlife rescue help. Nearby, a concerned adult and child stand ready to call, while the animal remains at a safe distance.
2
Introduction A baby bird hops along the ground, and its parents are nowhere in sight. What should you do if you observe a baby bird that appears to be orphaned? What if you discover a rabbit that seems unable to hop or a raccoon wobbling and swaying from side to side as it walks? Many people don’t know what actions to take, or not to take, when they find wildlife that may be in need of assistance. However, people with special training in caring for wildlife know exactly what to do. They know to leave the raccoon alone because it is probably sick, and they know whether they can help the rabbit and then return it to the wild. They can also look at the baby bird and determine what care to give, if Topsy, a three-month-old female any. These people can great horned owl, was rescued instruct others in how after she fell out of her nest during a windstorm. She is healing to help, too. from neck and back injuries.
Picture: A baby bird hops on the ground with no parents in sight, while a raccoon sways as it walks and a rabbit sits unable to hop. Topsy, a three-month-old great horned owl, rests in care after falling from her nest in a windstorm, healing neck and back injuries.
3
Wildlife Rehabilitators People who provide aid to sick, injured, or orphaned wildlife are called wildlife rehabilitators, or “rehabbers.” Rehabilitate means “to restore, or bring back, to good health.” Wildlife rehabbers rescue wild animals and care for them as they heal and then release them back into the wild after they recover, whenever possible. These wildlife rehabbers have special training in many areas. Some of them are nutritionists with expertise in what and how much to feed different species of animals. Others are behaviorists, meaning they have studied the behavior of wild animals and can understand and predict what a specific animal will do in various situations. They can also be animal-housing specialists who know exactly the type of cage, pen, or other enclosure to use for different species of when an animal is injured and needs a particular kind of shelter to heal safely. Animal caretakers treat a sea turtle for injuries to its right flipper.
Picture: Wildlife rehabilitators care for sick, injured, or orphaned wild animals. Some are nutritionists deciding food amounts, some study animal behavior, and others choose safe enclosures. Animal caretakers gently treat a sea turtle’s injured right flipper at a care facility.
4
Rehabber Lisa treats Tripod, Rehabber Lisa a young javelina who treats Tripod, a lost one of its back legs young javelina who when it was hit by a car. was hit by a car. Common Causes of Injury • Hit by vehicles • Hitting other objects • Poisoning • Illegal hunting • Litter and pollution • Animal attacks Many rehabbers can treat wild animals with illnesses and injuries, too, just as veterinarians do. However, not all rehabbers are veterinarians, and not all veterinarians know how to care for wild animals. Most rehabbers specialize in the capture and transport of injured wildlife, which means that they know how to handle wildlife safely to prevent injury to the animals or themselves. The work of rehabbers is needed more frequently as increasing numbers of people inhabit places where wild animals live. When land is used for farming, houses, and businesses, the natural habitats and foods of wild animals are destroyed, and animals are more likely to be orphaned, injured, or killed. People and their activities cause the majority of wildlife injuries.
Picture: Rehabber Lisa gently treats Tripod, a young javelina who lost a back leg after being hit by a car. The scene shows common injury causes around wildlife: vehicles, pollution, hunting, and animal attacks. Rehabbers handle and transport injured animals safely as people increasingly live near wild habitats.
5
Some wildlife rehabbers are generalists; they work with a variety of mammals, birds, and reptiles; but others are specialists, focusing in on just one group of animals, such as owls, bats, or ocean animals. Wildlife rehabbers often care for urban wildlife—animals that live in cities or towns, such as squirrels, raccoons, and foxes. Some courageous rehabbers work with dangerous animals such as bears, mountain lions, and bobcats. Most wildlife rehabbers work with animals that are indigenous, or native to the region where the rehabbers work and live. They have special permits and licenses to treat these animals. To work with exotic animals that have migrated or been brought to the region, a rehabber training with those animals as well as special licenses and permits. A Florida state wildlife worker examines a panther kitten.
Picture: A Florida state wildlife worker examines a panther kitten. Other rehabbers focus on owls, bats, ocean animals, or urban wildlife such as squirrels, raccoons, and foxes, while some work with dangerous bears, mountain lions, and bobcats. Many care only for animals native to their region and hold special permits and licenses.
6
Many wildlife rehabilitation centers specialize in working with particular species, although they communicate with colleagues who specialize in other species in case a different kind of animal is brought in that needs help. You might be wondering how people become wildlife rehabbers. Many rehabbers start as volunteers who learn how to care for animals in the homes and backyards of rehabbers with special wildlife training. Volunteers do not get paid for their work, but most rehabbers do not get paid, either. In fact, rehabbers often spend their own money to buy food, medicine, and shelters for the animals in their care. They are sometimes assisted by donations from people, businesses, and other organizations that also care about wildlife. A volunteer holds a baby red-tailed hawk while a People help wild animals rehabber takes off a bandage because they care about from a leg it broke when it fell out of its nest. animals and want them to get healthy. Rehabbers also recognize that proper care of wild animals promotes healthy habitats and prevents illness and injury to people.
Picture: At a rehabilitation center, a volunteer holds a baby red-tailed hawk while a rehabber removes a bandage from its leg, broken when it fell from its nest. Volunteers learn animal care without pay, and many rehabbers spend their own money on food, medicine, and shelters, sometimes helped by donations.
7
Rescue If you encounter an animal that may need rescuing, always follow these two rules: 1) do not touch the animal, and 2) call an adult right away. Adults can help by calling a wildlife rehabber who is trained in wildlife rescue. You can help by watching from a safe place to see where an animal hides so that rescuers can find the animal when they arrive. Putting a cardboard box with small holes or a laundry basket over an injured small An animal rescuer gives water to a koala he saved from a animal will protect it from wildfire. predators until help arrives. Watch Out! If you see any of these animal behaviors, stay away because they are clues that the animal may be sick and dangerous. • a bat on the ground • an animal that looks • a wild animal that appears to • a nocturnal animal (one that is normally active at night) • an animal with excess drool, or what appears to be foam that is active during the day— especially a raccoon, skunk, around its mouth opossum, fox, or bat • an animal that can’t move.
Picture: An animal rescuer gives water to a koala saved from a wildfire. Nearby, a child watches a small injured animal from a safe place while an adult calls a wildlife rehabber. The page stresses not touching wildlife and using a cardboard box or laundry basket to protect a small animal until help arrives.
8
SAFETY FIRST... for Animals • Animals may be injured or die from being held incorrectly when they are hurt and frightened. • Animals may be injured or die if kept in the wrong kind of enclosure. For example, wild birds may break bones or damage feathers when trying to escape from wire cages. • Human scent on a baby animal may cause its parents to abandon it. This is truer for mammals than for birds because mammals have a better sense of smell.... for People • Injured wild animals may be frightened because they are in pain. They may bite, kick, scratch, peck, or stab to try to defend themselves, not realizing that people are trying to help. • Wild animals may have diseases that can be passed on to humans and pets. Bats, coyotes, raccoons, foxes, and skunks are more likely than other wild animals to carry rabies, a deadly disease that all warm-blooded animals, including humans, can catch. Anyone who may have been exposed to rabies must be treated right away.
Picture: A frightened injured wild animal is held incorrectly and struggles, while another bird tries to escape a wire cage and damages its feathers. On the human side, a person steps back from a raccoon, bat, fox, or skunk because such animals may bite, scratch, or carry rabies.
9
Many baby animals are wrongly, and sometimes tragically, taken from their homes when they aren’t in danger. A baby bird hopping on the ground may not be orphaned—it might be a fledgling, a young bird learning how to fly. Fledglings often hop on the ground to practice moving before they fly and their parents usually watch them, though the parents might not be visible to people. People sometimes think they are rescuing baby rabbits, seals, and deer when these animals aren’t in trouble. It’s normal for babies of these species to rest quietly on a beach or in the grass while their mothers eat nearby. Only people trained in the natural ways of these animals know whether the babies need human assistance; if they don’t, taking them from their mother hurts their chance Unless baby animals are wounded, they of survival. do not need to be rescued.
Picture: A fledgling bird hops on the ground while its parents watch from nearby, and baby rabbits, seals, and deer rest quietly in grass or on a beach as their mothers feed close by. People sometimes mistake these healthy babies for orphans and take them from their homes, harming their chance of survival.
10
Many people wrongly believe they are helping wild animals by taking them home, but don’t confuse loving wildlife with being able to take care of these animals. Much more training is needed to care for wild animals than to care for dogs and cats—and besides, taking home wild animals is against the law. If you were injured in an accident, would you want just anyone taking you home and caring for you? No, you would want someone trained to help, such as a doctor or nurse. You would also want to be taken to a clean facility that had the appropriate equipment and medicine to help you. Like you, wild animals deserve the best care available. Veterinarians use medical equipment to examine a red panda. Do You Know? Spring is the busiest time of year for wildlife rescue because wild animals give birth in the spring and babies are weaker and often can’t survive on their own. A baby animal may become orphaned if a car hits its mother or if a hunter or predator kills its parents.
Picture: A veterinarian uses medical equipment to examine a red panda at a clean facility. The scene contrasts trained care with the mistaken idea of taking a wild animal home, which is against the law. Spring is the busiest rescue time because babies are weaker and may be orphaned when a mother is hit by a car or killed.
11
Rehabilitation When animals are brought to a rehabilitation center, the first step is to give them a checkup and first aid. Newly rescued animals are quarantined, or put in their own cages, to prevent them from potentially infecting the other animals. Rehabbers keep detailed notes about each animal so they can tell if the animal is improving, when to give it medicine, and when to feed it. Animals that come to rehabilitation centers can have a wide variety of problems. If they have broken bones or diseases, treat those problems first; afterward the animals can go to the to rest and get well. Igor, a black vulture, was fed birdseed instead of the meat he needed for good health, so his bones became fragile and broke during his rescue.
Picture: At a rehabilitation center, a newly rescued animal receives a checkup and first aid before being quarantined in its own cage. Rehabbers keep detailed notes on medicine and feeding. Igor, a black vulture, had fragile bones that broke during rescue because he had been fed birdseed instead of the meat he needed.
12
Animals may need to heal in one place and recover in another place. For example, a hawk with a broken wing might first need a small, dark cage where it can stay calm while it starts to heal. After the broken bone has healed, the hawk might need to move to an outdoor area called a flight cage, where it can exercise and relearn how to fly. Young, injured animals often need additional care so they can heal, and as they become stronger, they Rehabilitated birds practice flying in may be placed with an a flight cage until they have healed enough to be released into the wild. adult animal so they can learn from it. Adult animals show younger animals useful behaviors, such as how to hunt for food properly. Infant animals need special care because they need to stay warm. Rehabbers may put them in an incubator to keep them warm, or the rehabbers may place hot-water bottles, heating pads, or lightbulbs in cages.
Picture: A hawk with a broken wing starts healing in a small, dark cage, then moves to an outdoor flight cage to exercise and relearn flying. Young injured animals may be placed with an adult to learn hunting and are kept warm with an incubator, hot-water bottles, heating pads, or lightbulbs.
13
Most wild animals are frightened of people, and being away from home is stressful. Since too much stress can kill a wild animal, workers at rehabilitation centers try to protect animals from excessive contact with people by keeping noise levels low and covering cages with towels, among other techniques. They also refrain from staring at the animals because, in the wild, staring sends a signal that an animal is being hunted. A puppet serves as a substitute mother so that this young condor doesn’t imprint on the humans caring for it. Imprinting Baby animals imprint on their mothers at an early age; a baby duck learns that it is a duck by watching its mother every day. Wildlife rehabbers take special care not to let baby birds imprint on people; otherwise, the babies will grow up thinking they are human and will seek out humans instead of their own kind. Raptors, or birds of prey, and other birds that have imprinted on people may become dangerous in the wild. For example, they may seek attention from a hiker who doesn’t know they are used to human contact. The hiker or the birds could get hurt in the meeting, so for this reason, the birds often cannot go back to the wild.
Picture: A puppet serves as a substitute mother for a young condor so it does not imprint on the humans caring for it. Cages are covered with towels and noise kept low to reduce stress. A baby duck learns it is a duck by watching its mother, while baby birds must not grow up thinking they are human.
14
Do You Know? Meat-eating animals at rehabilitation centers need to eat meat so that they heal and grow strong. Wildlife up animals recently hit by cars or trucks along roads, called roadkill, to use as animal food. They also keep freezers full of “mouse-cicles”—frozen mice that they use as food. To save money, some centers A volunteer hand-feeds a mouse to an raise quail, mice, rats, and adult red-tailed hawk that is almost completely blind. rabbits as animal food. Wildlife rehabbers must feed each animal its natural foods or something similar because some foods can make the animals sick and hinder their normal growth. For example, cow’s milk is extremely harmful to many baby animals and can even kill them. Rescued animals must be fed natural foods so that they can survive in the wild later on. Natural foods are most important for orphaned animals that have grown up in a rehabilitation center. If they develop a taste for human food or do not learn to hunt or forage on their own, they might starve after being released in the wild.
Picture: A volunteer hand-feeds a mouse to an adult red-tailed hawk that is almost completely blind. Meat-eating animals at rehabilitation centers need natural meat, including roadkill and frozen mice, and some centers raise quail, mice, rats, and rabbits. Cow’s milk can harm or kill many baby animals, so rescued animals must learn to eat natural foods.
15
Release When a rescued animal is ready to return to the wild, rehabbers must decide where to release it. The search for an appropriate location begins long before an animal is ready for release. Knowledge of an animal’s natural history is essential for a rehabber, who must evaluate a potential habitat based on the requirements of the specific animal and the qualities of the location. Rehabbers find an area that has plentiful sources of food, water, and shelter, and they make sure the area is safe from human contact. Rehabbers must also be certain the animal is healthy enough before releasing it—that it can run, climb, swim, or fly without difficulty. They also make sure the animal is able to see, hear, locate food, avoid predators, and be social with other animals of its own kind. When releasing an animal, a rehabber will often request assistance from a wildlife biologist or ornithologist to make smoothly. This Tasmanian devil will be examined before it’s released.
Picture: A Tasmanian devil is examined before release. Rehabbers evaluate a potential habitat for food, water, shelter, and safety from human contact, and they make sure the animal can run, climb, swim, or fly, see, hear, locate food, avoid predators, and socialize with its own kind before letting it go.
16
The first step in releasing an animal is moving it to an outdoor pen or cage with other animals of the same kind. Once outdoors, the animal can get used to the weather and to less-frequent contact with humans until the animal is ready for release. Slow release is often used with young animals, especially orphans. Rehabbers put a pen in a safe place in the wild with the door left open so that the animal can return to it. Rehabbers provide food for the animal until it is clear that the animal can find food for itself. On the other hand, fast release is often used with wild animals rescued as adults because these animals already know how to live on their own in nature. They are taken to a release location, ideally near where they were found, and are let go. A group of onlookers watch as a wildlife rehabber opens the carrier’s door and lets this bald eagle fly free.
Picture: A group of onlookers watches a wildlife rehabber open a carrier’s door and let a bald eagle fly free. Before release, animals may move to an outdoor pen with their own kind to adjust to weather and less human contact. Young orphans often get a slow release with food and an open pen.
17
About half the animals at rehabilitation centers are too sick or too badly injured to ever be released back to the wild. Many animals have lost limbs, beaks, wings, or their eyesight. A bird with an injured wing that does not heal properly, or with only one eye, would not be able to fly or hunt in the wild. When it is clear that an animal will never survive in the wild or at a rehabbers have to make a difficult decision. If the animal is in pain, sometimes the best way to end that animal’s suffering. This adult female great horned owl is not a quiet flyer because of a wing injury. The noise warns prey animals she hunts, so she can never be released.
Picture: An adult female great horned owl rests at a rehabilitation center, unable to be released because a wing injury makes her flight noisy and warns prey. About half the animals at centers are too sick or badly injured to return to the wild; some have lost limbs, beaks, wings, or eyesight, forcing rehabbers to make difficult decisions.

Disaster Rescue Human activities are not the only causes of wildlife injuries. Natural disasters, such too. Terri Crisp spends much of her time rescuing are affected by natural disasters. During a wildfire in 1986, Terri rescued a Shetland pony Terri Crisp holds one of the animals she has by coaxing it rescued from earthquakes, fires, floods, into a car. She hurricanes, oil spills, tornadoes, and other disasters. worked to save sea otters and loons in 1989 during the Exxon Valdez oil spill in Alaska, and in 1992, she worked to rescue hundreds of animals hurt by Hurricane Andrew. She founded the organization called Noah’s Wish to train volunteers to rescue animals that are at risk because of natural disasters.

19
Incredibly, many wild animals knew to run inland before the 2004 Asian tsunami hit land; however, pets and farm animals did not fare so well. Several people in the areas hit by the tsunami depended on working farm animals; many of these animals were killed, injured, or left in devastated areas without food. The Humane Society International worked with other organizations and volunteers to help the animals—and the people who cared for them— get their lives back on track. A man carries his daughter and their dog to safety through high water after the tsunami in December 2004.
Picture: A man carries his daughter and their dog to safety through high water after the December 2004 tsunami. Many wild animals ran inland before the tsunami, but pets and farm animals suffered. The Humane Society International and volunteers helped animals and the people who depended on them get their lives back on track.
20
Conclusion Rehabbers often use animals that cannot return to the wild as ambassadors to help teach people to respect wildlife. Children and adults learn about wildlife that live in their local area and about respecting habitats so that wild animals can continue to find food and shelter. Wildlife rehabbers do important work in caring for injured wild animals and returning many of them to the wild. You can help rehabbers’ efforts by respecting wildlife and by calling a rehabber if you see an animal in distress. One phone call might give a wild animal the opportunity to grow up and live a free and healthy life. This bird of prey acts as an ambassador to teach children about animals in their area.
Picture: A bird of prey acts as an ambassador at a rehabilitation center, helping children and adults learn about local wildlife and respect habitats so animals can find food and shelter. Rehabbers care for injured wild animals and return many to the wild. People can help by respecting wildlife and calling a rehabber when an animal is in distress.
